# Open Threads

## Initial story threads

1. **The cracked toll bell:** Why does the Bellweather anchor respond to Aren, and what does its first crossing cost him? The local mystery pays off in Volume 1 — a damaged anchor that still remembers refusing a severance writ, a permanent roadmark, the remembered pitch of one verse, nine lost days, and three days of fever. The bell's provenance and its two-pence purchase stay live for the whole series, because at Chapter 900 it is silent under his hand.
2. **The living Bellweather path:** Bellweather-Living has its own people, debt system, and refusal to be restored by the main world. Established in Volume 1 through the guild, the toll houses, and the Hushwater ferry cooperative, and carried forward as a recurring relationship rather than a one-volume exhibit.
3. **The official Sundering account:** Who authorized the cull, what did Aren actually know, and why was his disgrace manufactured? Edric's name is put on paper in Volume 1's midpoint, by the toll houses rather than by the guild. The full Council context, the post-Sundering inquiry, and the cost of Aren's own silence are spread through Volumes 2, 6, 11, and 18.
4. **The Gatehouse's consent rule:** The archive was built to prevent forced historical selection, but the Council altered it. The First Witness is revealed in Volumes 11–15; Volume 01 may only show its *effect* — a private-keeper clause being applied to Aren in a provincial letter — and must not name the mechanism.
5. **Ilyra Kest's crossing:** Aren’s mother used the Gatehouse to carry him from a sealed path. Her route is answered by the final volume; her exact survival is intentionally unknown and is untouched in Volume 01 except as the verse whose pitch he loses.
6. **Aren and Mara:** Their relationship begins as evidence custody versus intrusion. Volume 1 stages it as a fight over a filing system, with her winning the terms of his only evidence. It develops through disagreement and changed decisions, never instant attraction or dependence.
7. **Aren's command flaw:** Volume 1 stages his first failure of the pattern inside the path (Chapters 6 and 41–47): a solo Bind that puts the force into the pier and hurts a man, because he is the best reader of the load path in the room. Volume 1's smaller version is volunteering for a cost nobody asked him to pay, in Chapter 7.
8. **Roads without Aren:** Established in Volume 1 by the guild bench, the path's stewards, and the refusal to be grateful. The limited channel that opens at the volume's close is run by a Merefen steward and a Bellweather-Living steward with one independent witness each; Aren witnesses and translates. Volume 18 can therefore remove him without removing the network.
9. **The named dead:** Ferris Oat, Hanna Vey, Corin Slate, Bess Alder, Anwen Rusk, and Bryn Rusk exist as records, not as figures. They are the reason the case has a moral weight the Crown cannot dismiss. Jos Rusk, who survives and claims for them, is a different person and must stay distinct from Pei Tarrow; both first appear in Batch 0002, never together.
10. **Hushwater's silence:** The drowned hamlet's name in older records hints that the Bellweather-Living path was known to the archive system before the cull. Volume 01 gives the hint its physical form — Hushwater Reach, a living hamlet on a channel that is drowned in the main world, with a crossing book in a living hand — and explains nothing. The cooperative counts hands and hearths, not souls, so its nearest estimate is about **sixty** and nobody can be more definite; the erased village of the same name is **eighty**, and the two figures must never be printed together or compared on the page. This stays a hint until Volume 09.

## Answers to the questions the outline phase had to settle

- **What exactly is Aren doing, and for whom, in the first three chapters before any crossing occurs?** He is holding a road at flood stage for a town that cannot pay him properly, in the cheapest labor available, and he is doing it because it is the only problem he is allowed to have. The first three chapters are one working day argued over by four parties with money at stake, his own bond, and the bell he should have sold.
- **What makes him ring the bell on the anniversary storm rather than any other night?** Three reasons, all concrete: the storm is the only night the river is loud enough to cover the sound of a working; the town's custom puts him among the ferrymen ringing for the drowned, so one man with a bar is nobody's story; and the Archive's deadline gives him a night he privately resents. The cracked bell is the hinge of a refusal made in a storm on that date, and it answers the same weather. **Prose note:** the Archive deadline is now the **second of Greenmonth**, the province's renewal date, and the nineteenth is the human date. Both deadlines point at the same week and only the first one matters to a filing system.
- **How does Mara first become involved, given that she works above a salt merchant and Aren works a bridge?** She needs a sworn tradesman's account of what the bell is attached to, because the Archive cannot record an anchor on a clerk's word, and the bell is the only surviving object she can put in front of a magistrate. She finds him through the Old Toll Bridge's parish repair marks, by asking the ferrymen who signed the sill. She offers custody and a claim number in exchange for a promise not to ring it — undated, and he agrees to the whole of it. He does not tell her the bell rings by itself, and Chapter 4 is therefore a broken promise rather than a technicality.
- **Which of the seven villages gives Volume 01 its most immediate human stake?** **Hushwater.** It is the only one with a living claimant in Merefen, and its crossing book is the document that can be checked against a ferryman's memory. Jos Rusk is the first claim Mara personally refutes, in Batch 0002, and he is wrong about the cause and does not want to be argued with. Alder Cross carries the second stake, through Bess Alder's parish list, which is what splits the Crown's four thousand back into two hundred dead who can be named. Volume 01 keeps the two figures on separate pages and never prints a combined total.

## Threads opened by Chapters 1–10

1. **The seizure was prepared in advance, and so was the second letter.** The bridge guild's removal notice is dated the eleventh, the morning it reaches Aren. Rell's sealed order is dated the **sixteenth**, before the storm and before the anniversary, and a second letter in the same hand reached the trust on the **twenty-ninth** — the trust, not the Archive — and again calls Aren the road-keeper of Merefen and summons him to the provincial town on the ninth of Greenmonth. The seal is the same hand-laid-on-a-book device on all three. Somebody in the marshal's office expected an answer from that bell, and expected two rounds of it. Volume 01 shows the dates and never explains them. Note that the ninth-of-Greenmonth summons is **already public** as of Chapter 10 and must not be used again as a discovery.
2. **The private-keeper clause.** Two provincial documents now name Aren the road-keeper of Merefen and the holder of the Bellweather hinge, and Lysa Fenn has searched the town's charter, the county's list of appointments, and the crown's own schedule of provincial offices and found no such office. The authority for the title is a hole, and a hole in a chain of paper is the plant for Volumes 02–03.
3. **The toll houses' northern trade.** On the page now: a toll-house steward in the guild hall using a foreign stranger as an argument, forty-one toll-houses, the three northern ones shut eleven years, and a promise that a register page might be had "in a year" from a man who has brought something to trade. That is the road into the toll-house counting room and the volume's midpoint.
4. **The outer ring of the second pier.** Eight inches, not growing this month, a new cutwater unaffordable, and a bench order to mark it twice a year. Carried from Chapter 7 to the volume climax, where the only way through is a public promise the debt cannot pay for.
5. **The seal match.** The wax on the path's counterfoil and the wax on Merefen's storehouse are the same press and the same hand, and Lysa has put that in a record in her own hand because it is the one fact in the file that does not depend on anybody's memory. This must survive to Volume 11's trial record.
6. **The register.** Named in the guild hall as the document that carries the signature the counterfoil does not, held by the toll houses. The index showing a second severance authorization in the same hand, four years later, in an unnamed county, is the door into Volume 02 and has not yet been seen.
7. **Whose name is on the eleven-year-old salvage filing?** Lysa Fenn was at this table and made a point of saying the bell filing and that filing are not the same document. She has still not said what the 301 filing covered, and the story still has not said.
8. **The bell's return.** Under a Crown seal in a watch storehouse by the fish weir, on a merchant's bell, in a town that is not the crown's. The town reclaims its own iron in Block 5; what the new band and the dull red sealing cost on the far side is a Block 5 question and not a Chapter 50 answer.
9. **Anchor-glass, four marks, a fortnight. — SPENT in Chapter 20.** The glass is bought, set and paid for; the money is a wage advance against Toban's cutting contract with a mark and a quarter of Aren's own in it; the maker's mark is under the near disc; and the maker's account of how it will fail is on the page. The open form of this thread is now thread 19 above.
10. **The verse.** The remembered pitch of one verse of Ilyra's river song is gone, taken by the bell's crushed note, and he will keep finding the hole. Untouched by the batch except as a wound; her route and her fate remain Volume 09 and Volume 18 business.
11. **The path is running without him.** Six days in, a crossing booked at the seventh hour, a crack being measured twice a year, a bench that has asked for a promise to return, and no road to carry a word. Batch 0002 must show Bellweather-Living moving on its own, not waiting.
12. **One bell in two places, and nobody can say why.** Chapter 7 now states it outright: nine stone, split lip to shoulder, the founder's mark on the shoulder is the Bellweather founder's mark read in a Merefen scrap yard — the bell is under a tarred cloth in Bellweather's yard and on an iron cradle on a bridge abutment in Merefen, and he rang it in Merefen, and both are true. The Crown's own clearance entry in Merefen reads *unclassified salvage*, with no city and no river, entered in **YR 302**. **Nobody in Volume 01 explains it and no character may be made to explain it.** It is not a time-diverge, a ghost, a twin, or a mistake, and it must not be written as one. The bell is an anchor and an anchor can be in more than one place at once; a reader who is told that plainly will stop treating the volume's hinge as a plot hole.

## Threads opened by Chapters 11–20

13. **The hole under two Crown seals is now a dated public record with a magistrate's name on it** (continuity item 55). The Crown can move a bell anywhere in Neyra and no office in Neyra can say who told it to. **The mechanism to use this with:** any office that acts in the matter again must answer for the last one, and the answer is dated. It is worth nothing until a provincial clerk finds it by accident.
14. **The county's emergency power, renewed annually to officers nobody can name** (items 55, 57). The renewal is a provincial fact a county magistrate cannot touch, and the finding that it is the real subject of the hearing is on the page in Lysa's own mouth. The clerk at the provincial court has a file on it. This is the road to Volume 04's road-shut block and to Iselde Senn's expansion of emergency powers.
15. **The YR 302 clearance entry, out of place in a bound order, in a hand the same age as its neighbours, filed unargued with a copyist's attestation** (item 59). A Crown roll in this county has been written to. **Nobody explains it in Volume 01 and no character may.** It is worth something to one person in about two years and the Archive does not know who.
16. **The missing-anchor ledger and the gap on a shelf in the fourth press** (item 61). The book is entered in the roll, was put away by Halven's own hand in YR 309, and is not there. It carries a schedule for **three bells on the Alder Vale crossing.** It opens on the **fifteenth** if Lysa Fenn's order is returnable then, and it does not open before. Halven knows it exists and is not the villain. **The volume's next-volume hook is a registrar quoting a fee for a missing-anchor ledger that should not exist.**
17. **A sergeant's statement naming a man in a stair** (item 54). The eel-man of the wharf road is selling the fever for a fortnight's worth of market and the court cannot un-sell it. He is wrong about the arithmetic of a Crown's convenience and right about it at the same time, and he is not in the file by name. Ottery is relieved, unpaid, and in Merefen.
18. **A quarter-inch datum** (item 58). The Sedge Reach bench mark is low and is now in the Archive's book on a carpenter's hand. Nobody wanted it and it governs twenty years of levels in this town.
19. **The anchor-glass, bought, set, and paid for** (items 62–64). The maker's account of **how it will fail** is the batch's most useful new document, because it is the only one in either world that says in advance what a glass road does. **A scar takes two and will not take three**; the third crossing is Block 5 and the sill's failure is what transmits it.
20. **A return stitch prepared with a record instead of a person** (item 64). Three hands, a date, an hour, and a copy in a press outside Crown custody. It has never been done before and it is the first thing in the volume that could be described as a stitch. **It has never been done with a person, and Chapter 22 is where that becomes the problem.**
21. **The unanswered ninth-of-Greenmonth summons** (item 65). A gap in a provincial ledger. In a year it is a fact about him instead of an argument, and a court that notices a gap will sometimes do something about it.
22. **The Market Square, which is not a square** — a board chalked every morning by a clerk, in a small hand, with a rule above each notice, where a line without a rule is a thing somebody put there on purpose. *Bell taken by the crown on the 19th. Nobody asked. Ask Kest, he was there* has been on that board since the first of Greenmonth. A board is a document a town keeps without knowing it is keeping one, and by the ninth of Greenmonth it will have the ruling of the fourth, a blank sum line, and a name on it.
23. **Pei Tarrow's mother, four miles out** (item 66). A Hushwater Tarrow, sixty-eight, alive, who was not in the village's book because she left it at nine years old, and who has spent eleven years being not noticed and asked her son to stop coming into town. Nobody has compared her existence to anything. Held for Volume 09.
24. **The bell report as a document** — an hour, a direction, a tower sight-line, three witnesses, and no conclusion, filed in the Archive on the eighth of Greenmonth as *reported*. It is worth nothing to any court for five years and cannot be argued with. The tower boy is thirteen and will be asked again in a year and will remember it in shapes.

## Threads opened by Chapters 21–30

25. **A name in a creditor's book, and a press-mark** (continuity items 73–76). The outgoing writ register names **Marshal Edric Hunn** as the signatory of the severance of "the Alder Vale crossing and its dependent places," nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301, and carries **press four, die eleven, impression two of the season** — the same press whose device, a hand laid flat on a book, is in wax on a post in Merefen and on a watch storehouse door. **The name does not leave Bellweather-Living in this batch and is not in any document in the main world.** The reader has it; the Crown's file does not. It reaches a court in **Chapter 46**, through the scheduled crossing, and enters the Archive as a claim in **Chapters 48–50**, exactly as `outline/volume-01.md` plans. Nobody is to accelerate this.
26. **A name in a creditor's book is a war** (item 76). The reason the page has not been produced in eleven years is not secrecy, not the Crown, and not a clerk's fee: it is that the book starts asking what the name was owed, and forty-one houses, a bench and an abbey would not survive the answer. This is the volume's political engine for Block 4 and the reason a Crown cannot simply be handed a document.
27. **The third hand.** The entry at the withdrawal was made by a third hand, not the deputy-clerk's, and it is **the only fair copy in four hundred leaves**, written by a frightened clerk. A young clerk in the counting house is the grandson of that clerk. Nobody has explained the third hand and it is worth something in a later volume.
28. **One load of flour**, carried off the north quay in the twelfth month of YR 301 and entered as the city's because the man who took it was in a Crown cloak and there was nobody on the quay who could say otherwise, and it is entered. The counting house will not correct it, because correcting a line makes the office a party in a shed with a lock on it. **A small wrong document that is deliberately left standing, and the whole argument of the book in one line of a business book.**
29. **The register's own index** shows a **second severance authorization in the same hand, four years later, in a county no one in Merefen can name.** This is the Volume 02 door, it has not been seen, and nothing in this batch may show it.
30. **The fourth press's shelf register, with a removal date and no name** (item 84). *The nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301. Removed.* A registrar disclosed it unasked, a copyist attested it, and the Archive has entered an application against nobody resting on it. Worth something to one person in about two years and the Archive does not know who. **Nobody explains it in Volume 01 and nobody may.**
31. **The agreement and its two counterweights** (items 77–78). A consent with conditions, a named steward (**Sera Quen**, the ferrywright — **not** the woman who keeps the Hushwater crossing book, who is a different woman and refused him a copy in Ch 23; Ch 27 now says so in a hall, and item 88 separates all three of them), a visible road, a promise with a date, two witnesses, one record. The price: **the third and last crossing of the year, pledged to a date nobody can promise he will meet, and a new anchor-glass seat that nobody has cut and that there is no fresh Steppe glass to make before Longlight.** If he is not there, she writes *not here*. This is the object Block 4 makes the reader wait for and Block 5 pays off.
32. **The second pier's marking**, twice a year by two men and a clerk, at the lowest water, in the middle of the year. Repointed in soft clay on the local method over Aren's objection. Still eight inches. The eighth-inch crack and the repointing are the two halves of Block 5: **a crack that moves because it is allowed to, and a bench that promised to hold it.**
33. **The third bay of the gate bridge** (item 81) — a third of it is not there, and the gate bridge is sinking on the town side, and the guild has no oak and no money. This is the ordinary failure the big one rhymes with, and it is where a city that cannot mend its own arch mends a smaller thing.
34. **The Crown letter chalked *not answered*** (item 72), in a drawer in the Merefen toll-house, and the **Market Square board with two ruled lines and three unruled**. Both are documents a town keeps without knowing it is keeping one, and both are legible to anybody who looks. Block 4 is where they are read by somebody with a grievance.
35. **The Archive is now a party and the second memorial is coming.** Mara has entered a claim number on her own account against nobody, and a registrar has disclosed a Crown press register unasked, and the county's man sent a memorial on the twelfth that this office answered by being dull. She has costed her own exposure in four items and paid all four. **A second and better-written memorial inside a month is the standing expectation she named out loud**, and a memorandum about this office's keeper is already in motion in a provincial book. This is the road to Volume 04 and to Iselde Senn.
36. **The bell-shaped hole in the Old Toll parapet**, a Crown seal on a post that has not run since the nineteenth of Mudmonth, a cradle gone green, and a seat cut square in the fourth course with two discs in it that have now been used twice and are past the number a glasswright will sell. The town reclaims its own iron in Block 5; what the new band and the dull red sealing cost on the far side is a Block 5 question.

## Threads opened by Chapters 31–40

37. **The letter, and the fifth piece that is not in the chain** (continuity items 102–103). Edric Hunn's first letter is dated the **eighth of Longlight** and arrived in Merefen on the **eighteenth**: eleven hundred words, accurate, no offer, one imperative sentence that needs no court, and a courteous request for the names of everybody he has spoken to. It is signed, so the marshal's name is now in a room in Merefen for the first time, and **it cannot go into the Archive's press, the magistrate's hands, a guild roll or a yard's book**, and it is not going into a chain, and there never will be one. He showed it to nobody, and the keeper worked out that he had it and entered the fact of its delivery and not its contents. **The letter goes to a court in Ch 46 or in the Block 5 resolution; the piece in the room over the cooper is the batch's standing cost and Block 5 should make somebody ask about it.**

38. **"Who else has he told"** (items 102, and the memorandum of the twenty-fifth). The Crown has every date, every room and every hand, and **no names**, because the only list in the county is the Archive's attendance column and the Crown read it through a *particular* in a provincial filing room. The names are the last thing not held, and a provincial clerk has concluded that the question is no longer about this county and has asked another office, and **that office has a woman in it and that office has a form.** This is the road to Volume 04 and to Iselde Senn, and it is also the road by which Mara's office is now formally an office.

39. **A writ of enquiry into a record office, returnable on the ninth of Rainmonth** (item 98). The Crown's own successful instrument, and it succeeds by not wanting to: for thirty days the Archive may not move, copy out, or part with any record it holds, and on the ninth of Rainmonth Mara answers a form in a court in the county town, in the fair hand, in the printed spaces, with no blank in it, and lists a **lending** that names a tradesman of Merefen and a date. **The answer is the next batch's clock.**

40. **The quarter inch on a Crown's schedule** (item 96). The Sedge Reach bench mark has been re-cut by hand three times in thirty-five years, always downward; every level in the county is out by a quarter of an inch in the direction that makes a deck look sounder, and **the stone is out a quarter of an inch and the nine inches is in the heads** — a man who takes a level off the mark writes a round number, because the book says *re-cut in hand* and not by how much, and the next man rounds it again, and four generations of men rounding in their own favour is a hand's width. Nobody will ever find a quarter of an inch on a mark a bench has dressed by hand, and the four generations is why the figure in a young man's head is. The figure is now on the county's own sheet of the second of Longlight in a remarks column with a date, an hour and a witness, and it is going into a provincial book. **The guild's roll still does not have it and the guild's man still refuses it, and that refusal is right.** Somebody in Fallowmonth is going to build to a level taken off that mark, and the book that says otherwise is in a leather tube.

41. **A column headed *Keeper*** (item 97). A Crown instruction to the watch bars the assembly of any body of this county in respect of the object, which means the next time the Merefen bridge guild wants to sit about the Old Toll crossing, a watch captain has to decide whether to let it. **Aren is on the guild roll and will be in that room in Frostmonth, and Rell will be outside it.** Held for Block 5 and for the bench's decision in a year.

42. **The report and the two readings** (items 104–105). A number off a mark in a coping, eight and then eleven, on a second pier, twice a year, entered in a filing room on the twenty-first of Longlight with five limits and the fifth of them being that the description is capable of being read of more than one bridge in more than one country. **The reading is not to be resolved silently.** If it is resolved, it belongs on the page and it belongs to a person who is not Aren and is not Mara, and the Crown's marking of a crossing its own registry says was never built is a different and larger door from the bench's own marking of a ring.

43. **The cold reading and the warm reading** (item 105). A ring stone opens in the cold and shuts in the warmth; the bench marks twice a year and has never compared its two numbers; the number four years ago is in nobody's book in this world. **This is the trade fact that Block 5's low-water exposure stands on, and the second pier is marked on Wednesday the twenty-sixth of Longlight whether or not he is there.** If he is not there, she writes *not here*, and the eight-inch figure stays the last one anybody in the main world knows.

44. **A chain in three places and a fact in a fourth** (item 106). Four dated pieces in a creditor's chest, a press by way of a lending, and a yard's week-book that holds the fact that a paper exists and cannot produce it. The spine is **a phrase a county clerk had to write into a printed space in order to refuse an order properly**, and it is the only piece of Crown paper in Merefen that helps the town. **The chain is the block's whole argument in one object and the thing Chapters 41–50 has to keep alive: an agreement kept by other people after the man who made it is not available.**

45. **The marshalled letter's no-names** (item 102) and **the petition's second column** (item 99) are the same document seen twice, and the chain runs from Chapter 32 to Chapter 38 in one shape: **a keeper's method is what let the Crown build a complete file without a single person's name in it.** That is the argument of the volume in a sentence and it is not a mistake by anybody; it is what a filing system is.

46. **The bench-minute of the fifteenth of Longlight, voted on a Saturday, and the returnable order the county would not carry** (item 100). Eight men and a reeve voted to keep a crossing they do not own at their own cost, and minuted that they took no notice of a Crown's paper, and a county clerk's refusal put the name of an office into a guild hall for free. **Both documents are now in the chain, and both are about a Crown's office that does not exist, and in about a year a provincial office is going to ask what the bench's minute is for.**

47. **A town which cannot pay anybody is a town whose men are free** (Ch 31, and Toban's market in Ch 33). The road is shut, the trust has nothing in it, the wages come out of a factor's paving stone, and four men in a yard are being paid at a rate on a Friday with a third party's name in the second column. What they do with that is the volume's question and it is not answered in this block. **Held for the guild's decision in Frostmonth and for Volume 02's labour argument.**

48. **Toban's rule, now tested** (item 106): *nothing that belongs to a man goes in this yard's book, and only a thing set.* It kept a document out of a yard on the day it was needed, and it produced the fact that the yard is worth having. **A rule that has been tested once can be broken once, and Chapter 40 is the first thing in the volume that has put a document's existence in a yard's book at all.**

## Questions Batch 0002 inherited and answered

- **All six answered in Batch 0002.** The day map (first / second / fourth / sixth / seventh / eighth, with the fifth a stated cut and the ninth belonging to Chapters 21–22); the refuted Hushwater claim, Ch 12; the sergeant who answers in writing and pays for it, Ch 15; the registrar's fee and the missing-anchor ledger, Ch 14; the glasswright and the price, Ch 11 and Ch 20; and nobody being able to find the road back, Ch 18 and Ch 20.
- Chapter 11 opens on the **first of Greenmonth**, the day after the batch ends. The province renews its finding on the **second**, and everything entered after it is a year weaker. The hearing on the sealed seizure order is the **fourth** — three days after the batch opens, two days after the renewal. Count from the day map in the Batch 0002 prompt and not from a remembered phrase; an earlier version of this line said "four days to the second," which is unreachable from any date the prose has established.
- The first surviving Hushwater claimant has a claim the keeper will personally refute, and he is alive in Merefen and does not want to be argued with.
- The watch captain's sergeant has to answer in writing for what the watch did, and answering is what costs him.
- The registrar who holds the Crown's own Bellweather entries has a price for a ledger that should not exist, and has already been paid once by the keeper of this office.
- A glasswright in Merefen sells honest anchor-glass at a price Aren resents.
- Nobody can find the road back, and the man who went there is the only man anybody would ask.

## Final-volume questions

- Can the Gatehouse be opened to thousands without making one person's memory the lock?
- Can path communities share roads while retaining the right to refuse contact?
- Will Aren surrender the private power that makes him useful?

## Questions Batch 0003 inherited and answered

- **The day map** (ninth of Greenmonth, the bench, the summons, the nineteen and the six): Chapters 21, 22, 29 and 30.
- **The register page and the name on it:** Chapter 26, in the counting room, with the page staying in the city.
- **The city declining to be a case:** Chapter 27, from an archivist, in front of two hundred people.
- **The admission, extended rather than filed again:** Chapter 28, in the guild hall, with the field book on the table.
- **A witness instead of a rescuer:** Chapter 22, from the bench-clerk, and Chapter 28, where the price of being witnessed is named.
- **The six days of Merefen without him:** Chapter 29, entirely in Tovan's view, and the vote that happened in it.

## Planning status

Volume 01 is outlined in `outline/volume-01.md`. Chapters 1–10 are written and carded in `outline/batches/volume-01-batch-0001.md`. **Chapters 1–40 are written.** Chapters 41–50 remain planned only, at outline-block level, and are carded in `workspace/volume-01/batch-0005/PROMPT.md`. Everything above the "Threads opened" headings is a planned promise; everything below it is an event established on the page in Batch 0001 and the batches after it.


## Threads opened by Chapters 41–50

49. **A scar, and what one is for** (item 116, Ch 49). *A crack is the stone telling you it cannot go on. A scar is a place a thing came through and can come through again.* The Old Toll fourth course is open, in register, and warm three times in five months, and the guild's man with the levels is going to take a reading off the Sedge Reach mark in the spring because it is the season, and nobody has compared that mark's history with a ring four hundred miles off. **The ordinary question about a scar is what you do with a road that has one in it, and the ordinary answer is that you mend it and keep it and tell the men who come after you where it is.** Volume 02's first road job is a dressed block and forty yards of hemp in a parapet at the bench's cost, and there is no money in Merefen for it.

50. **Two books that are the same book** (Ch 40's fourth limit, closed in Ch 47's notebook). The main-world bridge at a toll-house on the northern road, marked twice a year by a Crown's office with a rule and a boy with a book, and the ring in the other city marked twice a year by a bench at the two low waters with two men and a clerk. **The difference between them is a quarter of an inch of weather and a rule held by two men.** Item 105's trade fact is now applied on the page: the eleven cannot be the path's ring, because that ring's two readings differ by two tenths, not by three inches. **The Crown's registry keeps a schedule of marks on a crossing at a toll-house on the northern road, and the volume that contains the schedule of that registry is entered on a press register as removed on the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301, with no name and no office.** That is now the single largest documentary hole in the case and the provincial end of it is a clerk who reads press registers.

51. **The oaths, and the release** (item 119). The four-generation oath of the crossing was renewed on the twenty-ninth of Longlight before two hundred people and failed in five days, and the **narrower oath releases every person in that city who has been asked for a promise about a stone in the last eleven years.** That release is on a page in nine sides of minute in a hall made of drying floors, and it is a document in this world now, and **what it releases is a class of promise rather than a person, and a lawyer in a provincial office could read it as a waiver by four generations of a city.** Nobody in Merefen has it. It is a Volume 03 and Volume 11 question and it is worth something in a trial.

52. **The nine-month cutwater, against a pledge that was let go eleven years ago** (item 119). Alis Merrow has a grandson of nine whose name is on the front of a four-hundred-leaf book, and **the debt that the cutwater would be added to was released by a withdrawn writ on the twenty-second of Mudmonth, YR 301, and the man with the mallet worked that out standing up in a hall a year ago and the bench has not done anything about it since, because a thing that is bad for a creditor is very bad for a city.** The bench of nine is therefore repairing a crossing it is not the owner of, with no money, under an oath it has just narrowed, and it will be asked again in the spring. **Volume 02's labour argument is this bench.**

53. **The name on a separate sheet** (item 122). **Edric Hunn is in a county file in a sentence, on his own request, and the name is on a separate sheet in a press over a salt merchant under a separate number, and a court may read the two together and a clerk may not.** The list of persons the Crown does not have — the letters' four descriptions and no names — is now shorter by one, because the marshal has been told, in a minute, in a room, that the answer is no. **The remaining three descriptions are still unnamed in any Crown file, and the woman is the only one of the four who is in a provincial file by name, and she has been in it since Greenmonth.**

54. **The bench-clerk's question, and the two names at the foot of a sheet** (item 120). *The oath says the stewards of the crossing shall answer for it and shall answer first, and who is the steward of this crossing?* It was asked at the ninth hour of the thirtieth of Longlight in a room with five men in it, and it was answered at the ninth hour of the first of Fallowmonth by two people putting their names at the bottom of a sheet, in an order, neither signing the other's line. **The bench of nine is not the steward of anything. The ferrywright and the man with the mallet are, by name, on a piece of paper, and the toll-taker at the north steps is the witness and is not the steward, and a road warden in a fen county is none of the three.** This is the volume's whole argument in one sheet and it is now on both sides of a line.

55. **The Crown's letter in the room over a cooper** (item 103, unresolved). It still has no chain of custody. **In Chapter 50 a marshal stood in a county court and said that he had come four hundred miles and been right and cheap for the first time in a year, and nothing in that room was about the letter, and a woman in the same room who has known for four months that he has it and has never once asked has now had him in a room where it would have been easy to ask.** She did not ask. **The letter is the only document in this county with no chain on it and it is now the only one whose existence both halves of this business know, and it is the reason the question has moved from *will you stop* to *who else has he told*, and the answer to that is a form in a provincial office with a column headed *Keeper*.**

56. **The three hooks, and they are all in the same hand** (`outline/volume-01.md`, next-volume question). **What other paths are being sealed, and who is buying the records of their disappearance?** A claimant's form at the Archive about a fever in the eastern counties which has already produced a list of names to be removed from the public roll — the first thing in eleven years that has arrived on somebody else's initiative, and it is not this office's war. A registrar's fee quoted for a missing-anchor ledger that should not exist. And **the writ register's own index, which shows a second severance authorization in the same hand, four years later, in a county no one in Merefen can name** — a page of that register is now in a claim in a filing room over a salt merchant and a fair copy of a fair copy is in the counting house's own press in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists, and **the visibility of that index has gone from nobody to four people in two countries.**

57. **What a town's sentence about a man does, and the two versions of a bridge** (Ch 39, closed in Ch 40, reopened in Ch 44). The Market Square board has four ruled lines and four that are not, and the clerk has stopped wiping the lower half, and the last line says *Ask Kest. He knows what the oath is. He has not been here since the twenty-sixth* — and it is wrong, and it is wrong in the correct direction, and **in the last week of Fallowmonth the clerk wiped it and the town wrote *Road warden was here. He did not say.* and a bench put a block into a parapet at its own cost without asking him.** A board is a document a town keeps without knowing it is keeping one and this one has now been read twice, by a man from the north and by a bench.

58. **A stranger nobody can account for, and the only document that holds him** (item 118, Ch 47). A man of thirty-one or thirty-five, from the upper valley, who has been at the fourth camp for a fortnight cutting setts and whom nobody in a city of four thousand has asked where he came from, **and whose name is entered in the bench-clerk's book in the fair hand with the hour, and whose back a stranger opened with a boat, and who is the standing stone's keeper's neighbour on the north steps and recites the two hundred and eleven to people who come to look at forty tons of stone.** He has no papers in any country. **He is the volume's quietest argument and he is Volume 02's first file.**

## Questions the Volume 02 close inherits from Batch 0005

- **Where do the seven hundred and twenty go, one village at a time, and who is sent to find out?** The eighth ruled line is still empty and the city is not entered on it, and the saltbrim weights are cited and not filed, and a woman has written down that she wanted to fill it. That is the whole of what Volume 01 bought and it is the whole of what Volume 02 has to be careful with.
- **Can a man hold a crossing without a constitution, and how long does that last?** He has four people in a county and he has said out loud that four people is not a constitution. Volume 02 is the volume in which somebody counts them.
- **What does a writ of enquiry cost a county that has more than one record office?** Two writs went down the same tube three days apart, a third was in the tube and not sent, and a county clerk wrote a note about the reeve's records on a sheet of county paper and put it in a drawer and then told the reeve about it in a corridor, out loud, in front of two men, because he is a clerk who does not leave things in drawers. There are four record offices in this county and the Crown has found one mechanism and two clerks who will not do it quietly.
- **What is a piece of anchor-glass worth when a town is a scar and not a hole?** The glass is gone, the maker is four hundred miles away and his page says three is not a number he would sell, and the road runs on a line and two hands, and the two hands are a carpenter and a ferrywright, and **neither of them can be in two places.**
- **And the volume's own question, unchanged and not to be moved:** who has the right to make a decision about a living city that survived an official cull — the man who can cross, the office that culled it, the archive that records it, the town that stands on the road, or the people who live there? **The answer Volume 01 gives is *the people who live there*, and the answer it gives to who may decide *for* them is *nobody, and here is the sheet with two names on it*, and the answer to who pays when a promise fails is *the people who keep the crossing, at their own cost, out of their own materials, on a resolution of their own bench, and a woman with a rule in a county that cannot pay anybody is a woman whose men are free.** That is now on the page in four books in two countries and it is not going to be withdrawn.

## Threads the Volume 01 close carries into Volume 02

**Cross-reference: `outline/volumes/volume-01-close.md`, and then `outline/volume-02.md`.** The close audited all fifty-eight threads above; this list is the short hand-off and it does not replace either document. Threads 1–58 above are unchanged and remain the record of the prose. What follows is what the *volume* did to them, in the order the next writer needs them. **The "now" column states where each thread lives in the Volume 02 outline, and no chapter of Volume 02 has been written.**

1. **The bell, its band, and its seam (1, 36, and 8).** On the Old Toll abutment, banded with the town's own iron, split sealed from both sides at the same hour by a Bellweather-Living steward's hands. **Rung on a named day by a boy and a ferrywright, and from the twenty-ninth of Greenmonth, YR 313, by a tile-cutter in a county four hundred miles off. Not Aren's, and he does not ring it.** (The date was the ninth in an earlier draft and is now the twenty-ninth in four places; the ninth is impossible and the twenty-ninth is day 208 and the volume's last day. See continuity item 148.) Never opened.
2. **The two Hushwater figures (10, 24).** Near sixty in the path, eighty in this county. **Not printed together, not noticed, not explained, in Volume 02 or any volume before 09.** This is the one thread the close names as a discipline to hold and a frequency to correct: Hushwater stays shut, and **one other gap — the disposition schedule's county column, and eight counties with a boundary oath on a stone — is opened halfway in Chapter 99 and left.**
3. **The brine weights and the roll (22 in `outline/volume-01.md`; thread 41 in `state/continuity.md`).** Cited and not filed, with **two day-book lines about them a year apart, four lines apart within the month of Fallowmonth, read together.** **Not filed, not exhibited, not built into a roll in Volume 02**, and the reason is written out again in Chapter 98 and **the reason has changed: a roll is a ledger, a ledger is a claim, and a county of four hundred miles has just demonstrated that a claim about people is not a record of them.** Chapter 892 is still the payoff.
4. **The eighth ruled line (thread 123, item 123).** The city's, ruled, empty, and the reason for leaving it empty copied in a county boy's hand of nineteen. **Still empty at the end of Volume 02, and the not-filling is now a decision made twice and written down twice, a year apart, and read together.** Four thousand is the Crown's and never goes on that sheet.
5. **The second severance (threads 29, 50, 56).** YR 305, a county no one in Merefen could name, *returned* empty, *answered* eleven days after the tender, **press four, die eleven, a later impression of the season.** **In Volume 02 it is entered by Alis Merrow into a record of his own counting house, in his own hand, at his own cost, in his own city — the first document in the series held by a person in a path, and not in the Archive's press.** The two halves meet in Chapter 894.
6. **The missing-anchor ledger (threads 16, 30, 56).** **Sold.** Halven took the marshal's endorsement and sold the *Anchors, schedules and dispositions, Alder Vale* to the Office of Provincial Registration. The endorsement is dated, numbered, **and cannot be withdrawn, because no procedure exists for withdrawing one.** What is inside is a schedule for three bells and a county column, and **it does not answer why anyone can cross.** Chapter 894 is its payoff and Halven is its witness.
7. **The YR 302 out-of-place clearance entry (thread 15).** Still unattacked, still unattacked by anybody, still attested by a copyist with no opinion. **Untouched in Volume 02 and it should stay that way for a volume; it is a slow fuse and Volume 02 has six better things to burn.**
8. **The private-keeper clause and the office that cannot be found (threads 2, 38, 41).** **The clause is now on the page in an office's hand on a printed form and nobody in Volume 02 understands it.** The office is named: **the Office of Provincial Registration.** The form is named: **Form R.C. 4, Return of Contact**, with a *Keeper* column and a fifth column headed *how often*. **The phrase *keeper* does three jobs in Merefen and the volume is not allowed to make a meal of it twice; it is a joke, an employment, and now a column heading, and Chapter 51 is the last time the joke is used.**
9. **The count (new, and it is the volume's political engine).** Four descriptions and no names in Greenmonth; **eleven in Fallowmonth, read out in a guild hall with a date and an hour; twelve in Frostmonth, because a man was elected.** And then a figure for the county as a whole, and a three-line dated reply. **The count is how a road gets closed without a keeper ever being named, and the answer to it is a keeper's method, and nobody in the room notices until it is too late.**
10. **The Crown's letter in the room over a cooper (thread 55).** Still the only document in the county with no chain of custody. **Untouched in Volume 02, deliberately, and the close names this as a structural debt rather than a defect: it is Chapter 893's exhibit and it has to stay in a room until then.**
11. **The bench of nine, the trust in abeyance, the tolls, and four marks and six (threads 46, 47, 52).** **The bench is asked a second time in Frostmonth, on the fourth, and the true thing said in the room that day is anticipatory — a bench that is asked three times and says yes three times is not a bench, it is a subscription — and it is asked a third time and says yes a third time on the twenty-fourth of Greenmonth, in the same paragraph as the bill.** That is Volume 02's labour argument in a guild hall, and the same argument runs in tile in a county of ash. (An earlier draft of this thread put the third asking in Frostmonth, which is the second; see continuity item 152.)
12. **The yard (thread 48).** Toban's rule holds and is load-bearing for a third time. **The yard runs out of work: five weeks at the start of the volume, a fortnight by Wolfmonth, a factor's stone gone, a wall job going, and a boy asking to be put off two days instead of three.** No bench votes this one.
13. **The witness at each end (thread 54, item 120).** **Bram Ottery stands as the witness at the Merefen end of a road he does not keep and has no office in**, and the crown of grey wax over a doorway at the back of the yard is still in no book. **On the far side the answer is a person the county chooses and Aren has never met, and in Chapter 88 that person is struck and the road does not open.**
14. **The narrow oath and the release (thread 51).** Nine sides of minute on a drying floor, releasing a class of promise and not a person. **Untouched in Volume 02. It is a Volume 03 and Volume 11 question and it is worth a great deal in a trial, and Volume 02 has no business spending it.**
15. **The cutwater against a pledge that was let go (thread 52).** Nine months of a bench's whole capacity against a debt released by a withdrawn writ in YR 301. **Untouched. The path in this volume has a hole in a river in it and it is four hundred miles away and nobody from Merefen is permitted to go and look at it.**
16. **Hushwater Reach's name (thread 10, 23).** Held. **Volume 02 opens the first of a second path and does not explain the first, and a reader who has been trained to find gaps is going to notice that the book kept score for nine volumes and is going to be given one gap halfway in Volume 02 instead.**
17. **A stranger nobody can account for (thread 58).** A man of thirty-one or thirty-five from the upper valley, cutting setts at the fourth camp, whose name is in a bench-clerk's book in a fair hand with the hour, and whose back a stranger opened with a boat on the first of Fallowmonth, and who is the standing stone's keeper's neighbour on the north steps. **He has no papers in any country. He is Volume 01's quietest argument and he is not in Volume 02, and he is not to be put in Volume 02, and the file is a creditor's fair copy in a counting house in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists.**
18. **What a town's sentence about a man does (thread 57).** The board had four ruled lines and four that were not, and the clerk stopped wiping the lower half, and a bench put a block into a parapet at its own cost without asking the road warden. **In Volume 02 the board changes function: a line goes on it that is a figure, and the figure is wrong, and the town believes the figure for a month, and the close names the change as the deliberate correction of a device Volume 01 used nine or ten times without varying.**
19. **Two things nobody in Volume 01 is owed and both are in Volume 02's hands.** **A road warden elected by eight hands and a reeve's on the ninth of Frostmonth**, who accepts the vote and not the office, in the roll, in a sentence. And **a form with a printed space headed *Function of the office*, filled with a founding's own four sentences, with the space above the ruled line left empty** — a body corporate can be sued and a keeper can only be dismissed, and nobody in the room has said that sentence and Chapter 93 does not say it either.
20. **The volume's own question, unchanged and not to be moved.** Volume 01 asked who has the right to make a decision about a living city that survived an official cull, and answered *the people who live there*, and answered who may decide *for* them with *nobody, and here is the sheet with two names on it*. **Volume 02 asks what a record owes the person it describes, and answers that a record owes the person it describes the right to contradict it — and that a thing written down can be taken from you, and what cannot be taken from you is being asked.** The book's question under it is unchanged: a man who can cross and cannot stop crossing. **Volume 01's answer was that he has to be one of the things other people can hold. Volume 02's answer is that he has to be one of the things other people can hold *while the holding can be audited, dated, and refused*, and that the count of the people is not the constitution and the form and the furnace and the bench are.**

## Threads opened by Chapters 51–60 (Batch 0006, Volume 02 Block 1)

Threads 1–20 of the close hand-off above are the plan; these are what the prose did. **No thread above is closed and none is rewritten. What follows is the batch's own list, in the order a writer needs it.**

21. **The private-keeper clause is in print and is still not understood (close thread 8).** Now on the page twice: the full four sentences as a marginal instruction on the reverse of every form of the R.C. series, read out loud in a gateway in Chapter 51, and Halven's confession in Chapter 57 that **he is the man who made the first question about it and has been unable to get anybody to look at it for two years and told nobody but a woman with a press.** Sabin Dain has asked twice in writing and been told it is standard. **Nobody understands it, including the reader's proxy. Volume 03 is where Aren learns what it is being read as, and Volume 02 may not let anybody understand it.** The one line that is now load-bearing for later: **a hand-laid-on-a-book device is in grey wax on a storehouse door in Merefen, on the back of a Crown's letter, in a magistrate's finding, and — as a mark on a fired clay tile on a coping in the County of Ash — a hand's width with a rope on it and no yoke, which from two hundred yards is a bell.** Nobody in Ash knows what it is. **No character in Volume 02 may explain it.**
22. **The three-week figure, printed, and the drift arithmetic (close thread 9 in the power ledger; item 163).** **The Gatehouse's own figure for a held road is now printed in an office's hand on the reverse of a Crown form, as a standard of measurement, and the office cannot say whose figure it is.** The word is spelled out once in Volume 02, in Chapter 51's notebook. **The arithmetic is done in Chapter 57, in public, in a notebook, ten chapters before the volume needs it: six days of absence against a printed warning of twenty-one is minus fifteen days, and the warning was longer than the absence, and the warning did not fail — he is what the figure measures.** Crossing A: twenty-six days' absence, five days' error. Crossing B: eighty-eight days' absence, sixty-seven days' error. **The error scales with the work. A later volume may widen it; a Gatehouse warning may not silently become wrong by months.**
23. **The palm's fourth warmth, dated (item 164).** **The twenty-fourth of Fallowmonth — the day the bell went back and the channel opened — and it is the only one of the four he did not cause.** An earlier state line said the palm was warm "four times in five months and never once in Mudmonth"; that clause was false against Chapter 47 and is struck. **The palm is a road and not a mark and there is no one he can tell, and the volume has added no melody cost of any kind.**
24. **The one in four who says the word, and the clerk who would not write it (item 162).** **Aren Kest has now said the name of the thing in a room with a clerk in it four times in eleven years, and the fourth time the clerk was a provincial clerk of a provincial court, and the clerk refused to write it down and refused to have it entered, and gave the reason that a man who keeps a word out of a file for somebody else is doing the other man's work and not his own.** The word is in no document in either country. **This is the first time the book has shown the refusal coming from the other side, and it is the sentence the whole series turns on, said by a twenty-six-year-old clerk in a gateway in a fen county on the seventh of Embermonth.**
25. **The press register, and the return that answers nothing (item 160, 161).** **A press register is a record of what a man said he did with a shelf, not a record of a shelf, and it is the most honest book in this kingdom and it is honest about the wrong thing.** Halven has written his two entries out from memory and will not let them into a returnable paper. **Rowan Petch has decided to file a return that answers nothing, leaving the purpose out in terms and asking the office to supply its own purpose in its own hand under its own seal.** Chapter 72 collects what he filed. **A returnable paper with a blank in it is read as *nothing to state*, and a question closed by a return is a question nobody has to ask again** — this is now the volume's second mechanism after the count, and the Crown has two.
26. **The second severance, read at last (item 158, 159; close thread 5).** **One fact, three written refusals, an empty county cell, an empty *returned* cell, an *answered* cell eleven days after the tender, and press four, die eleven, second impression of the season against first impression at the head of the same leaf.** The name is in the body line eleven pages away in the same book, **in a different hand**, and neither hand is a hand that signed anything. **The register has not been asked for and will not be.** The four people who can see the index are unchanged: **Alis Merrow, Aren Kest, Mara Vey, Rowan Petch.** **In Volume 02 the second severance is entered by Merrow into a record of his own counting house, in his own hand, at his own cost, in his own city — the first document in the series held by a person in a path, and not in the Archive's press.** Chapter 894 puts the two halves side by side.
27. **The shape of the holding this case needs, written into the day-book on the eighth of Embermonth and not executed.** **Mara Vey has entered that the only kind of holding that has ever answered a question in this case is a holding that cannot be served; that this office has one, and it is a room on a canal wharf at the price of a farthing of salt; and that it is not going to be used for this, because a roll of persons in a lock-up on a wharf is a thing a creditor could sell.** And then she has entered the requirement: **somebody has to make another one, in the country where the register is, out of his own book, at his own cost, on his own paper, with his own name at the head of it, in a matter in which no court in this county has jurisdiction, and this office cannot do it, cannot order it, and will not pretend that it can.** **This is the first rehearsal of the Charter of Many Roads and it is entered in a day-book and not in a document, and it is Chapter 99 that pays it, and it is a creditor who does it, and he will say in a hall that it is the worst possible thing.**
28. **Three empty cells in one week, and the eighth ruled line (close thread 4; item 158's chapter).** **The eighth ruled line in the served schedule is still the city's and is still empty, and she has now looked at it twice and wanted to fill it twice, ten days apart, and has written down both times.** The two wants are not the same want: the first was *I do not know what is in it*; the second is *I think I know*. **A cell a keeper wants to fill because she knows what goes in it is not a cell any more, it is a mouth, and she is the keeper of it, and the person who will be asked about the eighth line in nine years is her.** In the same week she has found an empty county cell in a creditor's index leaf four hundred miles off, and has refused to fill it, and has noted that she has been in one room with three empty cells in a week. **Four thousand is the Crown's figure and never goes on that sheet. The eighth line is still empty at the end of Volume 02 and the not-filling is now a decision made twice and written down twice and read together.**
29. **The eleven, and the count, and what it is for (item 156; close thread 9).** **Eleven, read out in a guild hall on a Saturday market morning with a date and an hour, entered in a day-book against no person and not on the guild roll, because a roll in that town has a *Keeper* column on it now.** Four of the eleven are in a county file by name and the figure of eleven contains all four and names none of them, and the *Keeper* column on the copy that went is blank because a form with a figure in it does not need a name in it. **The eleven are the first rehearsal of the count, and Chapter 75 makes the count twelve by giving a man a vote, and Chapter 76 is the day the count reaches the province.** **Nell Tace's eleven names in the eastern counties are a different eleven and are never totalled against these eleven.**
30. **The refused road, and the sentence (item 166).** **A road that is refused is worth more than a road that is open** — the toll-man on the north steps, in line ninety-four of a crossing book, written at the third hour and sent through with him. **A county that refuses a road has something to refuse it with, and this county has nothing and has never been asked; the county on the other side of the river has something to refuse it with and has never refused anything, and he does not know which of the two is in worse order and has been in both.** And the sentence the whole block is built on, said out loud on a bridge in a market town to a woman who asked him one question: **she did not open it and she was right, and I am not the man who sends anybody, and it made no difference, which is how I know she was right.** **The road did not open and it did not fail, and the far-end witness line on the return is empty, and that is the whole mechanism, and Chapter 88 is the day it is discovered that the same line can be emptied again by a person being struck.**
31. **A county refuses to be copied (item 167).** **The column headed *hand*, empty on purpose, twice refused, and the reason is the best sentence in the book: everybody keeps the road and nobody is named, and that is how you have a road.** And the corollary, from the man with the ledger: **once is a clerk, twice is an officer; a clerk is asking a question and an officer is establishing a fact.** He will not put a name in a cell and cannot leave a trade blank, because **he counts the county and not himself and has never in nineteen years been short** — which is the same sentence as Chapter 61's stock count and the same man, and **the reader has met him twice before the arithmetic and does not know it.**
32. **Ash's own calendar, and the plant for the breach (item 168).** **The paper of the eighth of Embermonth is dated the nineteenth day of the rolling, being the sixth day after the low water, the year counted from the first firing, and it is not this county's new year and nobody in Merefen can check it.** The arrival two days later is the twenty-second of the rolling, being the ninth day after the low water, and the low water is the thirteenth of that rolling. Aren has copied the date exactly into a notebook, in order, with the hour, and written under it that a man in a filing room is going to read a copy of that line in about seven months and will not be able to tell from the line which side of it the year was on. **Chapter 92 is the breach and it is found with a date and not with a question, and it is planted here, ten chapters early, in his own hand, by him.**
33. **The kiln clerk's *north* (item 167).** **The first entry in a county column in nineteen years that is not a place anybody in that county can walk to, written by a man who counts the county and not himself, off a stranger's answer.** His name is withheld on the page by design and the reader has not had it. **He is the man who says the number out loud in Chapter 61 and a room of nine stops what it is doing, and he is the county's introduction and he is not a mystery.**
34. **A county that has never been asked names itself without being asked (item 168).** **Four lines, two names, one date in an alien calendar, and the fourth line is the volume's argument in a gesture: we were asked a question by a stranger and we have written our answer down, and we do not know the answer, and the writing of it is the answer.** **A thing written down can be taken from you. What cannot be taken from you is being asked** — and this batch states that in a path community's own voice on the eighth day of the tenth chapter of the volume, and Chapter 87 says the same thing again in Chapter 87's own words and neither is a paraphrase of Merefen.
35. **The witness who keeps nothing (item 169; close thread 13).** **Bram Ottery stands at the Merefen end of a road he does not keep, for fourpence a week out of a private box that he will not draw, and gives as his reason that he would like once to stand somewhere while a true thing is being done.** He is one of the eleven, he asked for the eleven not to go on the roll, and he is the count's proof that the count is measuring the wrong thing. **He is elected in Frostmonth and the count goes to twelve and nobody in the room can prove it.**
36. **The reeve's one refusal, and her nine years (item 170).** **A road this town does not own cannot have this town as its witness, and if it has then the road will be shut by a signature and not by an argument, and an argument is a thing that can be had again on a different day by a different bench with a different temper in it.** It is the first thing anybody in that town has written down for her in nine years and Tovan Kest wrote it down. **She is re-elected in Frostmonth because nobody can produce a rule that keeps her off the bench, and she declines to vote on a thing she is witnessing in Chapter 77, and it is minuted.**
37. **The brother, named out loud, and not forgiven.** **The eighth of Greenmonth, a parapet, a lamp, a purpose read out twice and handed over to be signed, and not one question asked in four hours.** Tovan read the line he kept under a rule since the second of Fallowmonth out loud in a gateway in front of four men, and gave the reason for the two months since he wrote the line down, and the reason is *the day I ask is the day you have to answer.* **Aren said the reason was good and was not true, and Tovan said it was the reason he had, and nothing was repaired.** The yard has four weeks and a day in it. **He holds the near end of a line at the fourth hour on the ninth and nothing comes and he is not told why for twenty-six days, and in Chapter 91 the cost is that his brother cannot do the order of an ordinary morning any more and cannot say which order it was.**
38. **The small paid items, and what they are for (item 172; Chapter 56).** **A farthing of salt, and four pence a year, four times apart in price and about four hundred miles apart in kind, and both of them bought for a door.** *A door on a lane is the only thing this business has ever bought that a marshal of a Crown cannot serve a writ on.* **She would like somebody in about eleven years to compare that figure with the four marks and six a year this county pays a bench of nine to keep a bridge.** The Archive is now in one room over the chandler's at the north end and Tovan has already said that one room is two rooms' work and no more.
39. **The Crown's escalation in kind, and the two instruments that need no court (close threads 8, 9).** **Form R.C. 4 with a *Keeper* column and an undated copy that did not have to be answered; and a return asking for a count of persons rather than names, which is lawful, needs no court, expires for want of a party, and cannot be refused without refusing a number.** **Both are the same office doing the same job with two different instruments, and the office is not under the marshal and is more dangerous than the marshal, because it is doing its job.** Nobody in this county has yet said that sentence out loud. Chapter 76 is where the office proposes a count for the county as a whole in three dated lines, and Chapter 93 is where it cannot be touched at all.
40. **What the Volume 02 close inherited and Batch 0006 did not touch, on purpose.** The narrow oath and the release. The cutwater against a pledge let go. The YR 302 out-of-place clearance entry. The Crown's letter in the room over a cooper, still the only document in the county with no chain of custody. The brine weights, still cited and not filed, with two day-book lines four lines apart. The Hushwater Reach figure and the Hushwater figure, not printed together and not noticed. The two Hushwater women who are two women. The eight counties with a boundary oath on a stone, which is the volume's next-volume question and is now visible in a county column that a registrar sold and a keeper copied for nine minutes in a fair hand. **All of these are still where the close put them and none of them may be spent in Block 2.**

## Threads opened by Chapters 61–70 (Batch 0007, Volume 02 Block 2)

Threads 1–20 of the close hand-off are the plan and threads 21–40 are Batch 0006's list; both stand and neither is rewritten. What follows is this batch's own list, in the order a writer needs it.

41. **The four hundred and fifty-six, and the floor under it (continuity 173).** **2,906 tiles on the roll of persons, 2,450 people, 456 over, and the population is exact because Chapter 61 is a subtraction done out loud in a kiln shed in a minute and a half.** The reader can do it. **The four hundred and fifty-six is a floor of a surplus and not a best estimate, and no figure in this volume may be got from it by scaling it** — Chapter 66 says so in the notebook and refuses to give the remainder a number. Where the rest of it is, and will not be counted: **the presses.** A soft tile on a shelf in a house is not on a roll and not on a wall in a shed, and every house in this county has had a fire in it and not one of those fires is a kiln. Chapter 89 is where two thousand nine hundred and six tiles are re-ground; **the presses are what is underneath that and they are still uncounted at the end of the volume.**

42. **The missing column, and the blank nobody has noticed (continuity 180).** **The fifth space on a struck person's form is headed *struck* and takes a name, a date and a witnessing hand, and there is no sixth space, and the sheet is the same width it was the day the roll was begun, and nobody in the county has ever met the man who ruled it.** The sentence that cannot be put in a form — *a person who is struck has to be able to answer for himself, and if he answers he goes back on it, and if he cannot answer then he was never on it and we have been keeping a list of things* — is eleven months old as a thing somebody has said and has never once been a thing anybody could enter. **This is the same shape as the eighth ruled line in a served schedule in Merefen four hundred miles off, and no character in this block may say so and nobody in this block knows it, and the two documents are never in one room.** Term four of the five is the same sentence written as a road's condition, and term four is what kills the road.

43. **A soft tile can be re-pressed by anybody with a thumb and a wet cloth (continuity 176).** **A struck person is given one of the same soft tiles a child carries, and it is the county's only thing a person can still change, and it took a woman's whole account of herself in a winter because it had gone out of true on a shelf and her son-in-law fixed it before he went to work.** This is the load-bearing physical fact of the volume and it is now on the page in three places. It is also the disease's mechanism in miniature: **the thing that can restore a person is the same thing, in the same county, in the same county's ordinary housekeeping, the thing that can take one away,** and no law in nineteen years of this county touches a soft tile at all. Chapter 84's argument — a record that a person can change is a record, and a record that cannot be changed is a claim — arrives here first, in wet clay, in a kitchen.

44. **The seven days, and what the county actually did with them (continuity 184).** **About two hundred and six people asked, once, by a cord-cutter and an ash-driver in their own sheds, in about a minute and a half a house, the answer written in the asker's own book and shut, no copy made, no name into any office, and the stranger from the north carried boards for nine hours and asked nobody anything.** **It is the only civic work in this volume that nobody has ever written down, and it is Chapter 87's argument waiting to be said by somebody, and it is the rehearsal for the roll in three hands and it is not the same thing as the roll in three hands, because a thing written in a man's pocket and read at his own table is a mark and it will be read, which is exactly the physician's objection and it is on the page.**

45. **The county law's four lines, and the clause nobody has looked at (continuity 178).** *The clerk of the roll shall destroy the tiles of every person so named, and shall enter the destruction in the roll, and not the name.* **A line of county law that forbids a name being written beside a destruction, defended by a magistrate as mercy, in a county where a mark is the vector.** Signed by three magistrates, **the third of them nine months dead, and the reason nobody says a word is that a signature is a signature.** The list is not burned, at the sittings on the twenty-second day, **because the clerk of the roll said no and gave a reason about a wedging shed.** Chapter 98 supersedes the list and does not burn it, and this clause is why.

46. **The physician's four pages, read out loud once (continuity 179).** **Read out loud in a room with a table for the first time in four years, on the twelfth day, to a sett-cutter, with the rollkeeper standing in the doorway and the magistrate's copy on the table with two of the physician's corrections missing.** The finding stands: the softening enters by the mark and travels fastest along the marks read most, the marks read most are the roll marks, **the cure is not fewer records and the cure is records that do not disagree.** It has been misunderstood twice — cleverly, by the rollkeeper, and kindly, by the magistrate — and both misunderstandings are on the page and both are reasonable. **Chapter 88 breaks the re-reading up and makes it local and slow and runs it for nine days in eight villages and a market town with no office reading anything and no stranger in the room, and that is the county's own answer and it is the finding honoured and the rite refused, and it works because it was designed by people who had been cut off.**

47. **The shape refused, and the half he has and does not use (continuity 182).** **A promise about two hundred people to one office is a shape he has had refused before me by a man in a room in another county, on a paper about a number of persons in a place that could not be asked a question.** He has since found the office and the form and the column with a word at the top of it, **and he has half of the other half of it, and he has decided in writing to leave the rest alone, and the decision is in the notebook and is a decision and not an oversight, and nobody has ever read the notebook.** **Volume 03 is where he works the rest of it out and Volume 02 may not let anybody understand the clause.** The note that a clerk in Merefen will one day read a line and not be able to tell which side of it the year was on is now made **twice, in his own hand, in the same notebook**, and the second time is about a sum in two calendars and a week.

48. **The tile on the coping, twice, and still unexplained (continuity 183).** **Six inches by four, fired grey clay, a rope in two loops and no yoke, on the coping of the second span at the Ninebridge, a hand laid flat on a book, the rope about a year old and the tile older than anybody alive who has touched it.** Shown at four feet in daylight in Chapter 65, handled in his hands in Chapter 69, taken down for one morning and put back. **He lies to the only person in the county who has thanked him for anything in order to protect it, and the chapter says that is a debt and not a release, and he does not use the name on the paper of the eighth of Embermonth, and the reason is on the page.** **Chapter 85 is still the chapter that gives it its scene and Chapter 85 does not explain it either, and no character in this volume may.**

49. **Bel, four, and a tile nobody has looked at (continuity 188).** **A child at a board in the cutting shed at the north end of the Ninebridge, doing the check round on soft tiles, nine right in a row, smaller than he is and better at it for being small.** She presses her own tile wrongly on the third day and is put right by a stranger's one word, and puts it back in the fourth row and goes back to the check round. **Her name is spent and her tile is not looked at in this batch, and nothing any character in this batch describes any mark a child has pressed.** Chapter 86 is the chapter.

50. **The two refusals, and both are correct (continuity 184, 185).** **A roadkeeper on a bridge in a market town on the first morning, asking one question, and not opening, and not failing, and being right; and a room with a stove and a physician and a keeper of records in it, on the sixteenth day, refusing to take his advice, and being right, and the reason he gives for the refusal being the reason the whole volume is built on.** Between them they are the book: **a man who can be refused, and a man who has nothing to say, and a room that stops him, and a road that does not open, and a fever and a silence about a way of standing.** Neither refusal is repaired in this volume and the first is not repaired in Volume 03.

51. **The yard at eleven days, and a bench going to name a third day (continuity 186).** **Four weeks and a day less twenty-six days is three, and a wall off the Fallow Elms road that pays in wood because four men cannot be fed on three days' work.** The Sedge Reach scour is a washtub and has been since Mudmonth; the bench named the ninth of Embermonth and had it, named the twenty-fourth and had that, **and it is eleven days past the second, and a bench of nine that has named a day twice and had it twice is going to name a third, and the third will be taken alone by the guild's man with the levels and Tovan will have to believe it.** Chapter 97 is the bill and the third asking. **None of this is resolved in Block 2 and none of it may be.**

52. **The copying post is gone, and the nine shillings have no post to be in (continuity 185).** **A line from the county's surveyor, dated the third of Wolfmonth, not a letter: the post is not held and a man is to be put in it. No name, no day, no mention of the three Fridays.** Entered in a day-book against no person with the hour, **and not resolved, and the reason it is not is that the surveyor takes a man on in Thawmonth and the twelve marks and ten pence of Chapter 97 is sixteen weeks and two days of a post that was not there to be initialed.** A cage is a place a man is sent for and also a place a man is sent away from, and he wrote half of that difference out in Fallowmonth.

53. **The not asking, which is now a wound, entered in a day-book on the eighth hour of the fifth of Wolfmonth (continuity 187).** **A protection is entered in a file; this is entered in a day-book over a weigh-house in a room with a door on a lane at four pence a year, and the difference is about two months and she has noticed it late, and she has entered the day she noticed it on a page that has had nothing on it since the twenty-ninth of Fallowmonth.** **The thing she is not asking about is the way he stands.** **It is not resolved in Volume 02 and it is not resolved in Volume 03, and Volume 04's chosen intimacy is bought with it, and the breach proper is Chapter 92 and is found with a date and not with a question.** What is on the page now is a woman deciding in advance what she will not ask, in writing, with the hour, in a book that is not a record — which is the practice the whole series runs on and the first time it has been entered about a person instead of about an office.

54. **What the Volume 02 close inherited and Batch 0007 did not touch, on purpose.** The narrow oath and the release. The cutwater against a pledge let go. The YR 302 out-of-place clearance entry.
