The Crown's letter in the room over a cooper. The brine weights, still cited and not filed. The Hushwater Reach figure and the Hushwater figure, not printed together and not noticed. The two Hushwater women who are two women. **The second severance's index leaf and press-mark** — press four, die eleven, second impression of the season — which is untouched in Block 2, because nothing in a clay county is connected to a creditor's register in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists, and the reader has to hold the join. **The count, which is eleven and does not move in this block.** **The private-keeper clause, which is not mentioned, referred to or explained by anybody in this batch.** **All of these are still where the close put them and none of them may be spent in Block 3.**

## Threads opened by Chapters 71–80 (Batch 0008, Volume 02 Block 3)

Threads 1–20 of the close hand-off are the plan, threads 21–40 are Batch 0006's list and threads 41–54 are Batch 0007's; all three stand and none is rewritten. What follows is this batch's own list, in the order a writer needs it.

55. **A count of persons is a list with the names taken off, and the Archive has now returned one, and the office has answered a count with a bigger count** (continuity 192, 193, 194). **Eleven went to the province on the ninth of Frostmonth with the *Keeper* column empty and no reason entered, and the four of the eleven who are in a file are all in the figure and none is named, and the figure is lawful and it is not a list and it is the answer to Edric Hunn's question.** Four days after a carpenter wrote *nil* on the back of a week's wage sheet in Weir Street, the office wrote three lines in a woman's hand: *received. The office notes that a count of persons is not a list and proposes a count of persons for the county as a whole.* **Nobody in Merefen has read that sentence. It sat unopened in a day-book for twenty-two days because it asked nothing of that office, and a paper a keeper has not read is a paper she has answered, and Mara Vey entered that she is the only person in this county who has read both documents and that she does not see anything in them.** **Aren Kest is the only person in the book who could have said what it is and he was twenty-five yards from the door.** Chapter 93 is where the county as a whole cannot be touched at all, and Chapter 94 is where the woman who wrote it says the sentence about a figure having no name in it.

56. **A returnable paper with no party expires, and a paper with nobody in default is not refused, and a court can beat an instrument with the form's own construction** (continuity 195). **Lysa Fenn found it in nine minutes on the twenty-first of Wolfmonth and let the instrument run out on the ninth of Frostmonth, and the reason is on the file and is a construction and not a feeling.** And she wrote down, in her own book, why she is frightened of her own answer: **she has built the best answer in this county out of the fact that this court is not a person, and in about four years somebody is going to make this office a person, and then a person can be in default and can be fined and can be shut and the answer dies.** **Marda Oakes wants that to happen to the Archive on purpose, and has said the half of her argument that is useful and has refused on purpose to say the other half, and the other half is the sentence: an office with a door can be sued, a keeper can only be dismissed, and a town is safer with things it can sue.** Nobody in Chapter 76 says it and the chapter does not say it and the reader can. **Chapter 93's county council resolution is that sentence becoming a form with a printed space, and Volume 03 is where the private-keeper clause is worked out and it is not worked out here.**

57. **The eleven-year-old salvage filing, refused four times, in writing once, and with a fifth promised** (continuity 197). **She took it from him in YR 301 and told him in advance, three times, on purpose, so that he would not be surprised, and refused a signed request in writing on the eighteenth of Wolfmonth and put the reason on the file, and told him in the room that if it comes a fifth time she will answer it and has told him now so that the fifth is not a surprise either.** He was not surprised and said nothing, twice. **A later writer may make somebody ask about the fact of the filing once more and be refused. It may not be asked about more than once in the whole of the case, and the contents are not explained in this volume.**

58. **The bench's three askings, and the second one, and the subscription sentence that is now on the page about all three** (continuity 198). **Four marks three shillings and fivepence of work, four marks and six a year, two marks in a chest, two marks of notes promised, short by three shillings and fivepence, to be found by subscription and not by rate, and the whole of it to go in the market book with the names and the figures so that in nine years a man may go and see whether this town was asked or was told.** **And the true thing, said standing, with the level case down on the table, on the fourth of Frostmonth: a bench that is generous decides, a bench that is asked is being told what it is for, and a bench that is asked three times and saying yes three times is not a bench, it is a subscription — and it is anticipatory, and a man who watched it happen once four hundred miles off has put it in a minute while it was still early.** **The third asking is Chapter 97, on the twenty-fourth of Greenmonth, in the same paragraph as the bill and the fortnight with the road shut, and no character in the Chapter 74 room may call it the third and none of them does.**

59. **A reeve has a casting vote and a casting vote is not a vote, and a reeve who uses it twice in one meeting has crossed a line she held for nine years without noticing** (continuity 199, 200). **Nine years of not voting on the guild's own matter, and the mechanism of it is now on the page, and on the ninth of Frostmonth she cast the vote twice and the second one put a stranger in an office; and on the fourth of Hearthmonth she voted with her hand for the first time in nine years, and the clerk entered *the reeve voted* and read it back and nobody asked him to.** And then, in the same hour, in a gateway on Weir Street, she refused the witness line of a road in the words of the ninth of Embermonth, word for word, and said that a man may do one of the two and not the other, and it took her nine years to find out which of the two she would rather do and it is not the one she thought when she started. **She is the independent witness at the Merefen end of a channel and has declined to sign as one, and nobody has said why she was asked and nobody in the yard connected it.**

60. **A man accepted a vote and refused an office, and both facts are in the same minute, and he noticed the price of the refusal two seconds late and said nothing** (continuity 199). **Aren Kest is on the roll of the Merefen bridge guild, elected road warden on the ninth of Frostmonth by eight hands and a reeve's, and the fourth line of the minute is that he accepts the vote and not the office, and the volume's last sentence is *I do not intend to be the man who holds this crossing*.** **He will have a vote on a question that is not about him. He has found out that a refusal had cost him nothing, because the price was on the man afterwards and the price was a bench that had learned his name was worth a vote — and he has not said it, and the reason is that a man who argues in a room that he ought to be disqualified from a vote has asked the room to do his arithmetic.** **Chapter 100 has a road warden on the Sedge Reach deck with a tool bag who is not holding the crossing and has not resigned and will not.**

61. **The count of twelve, and the fact that the two numbers are not the same count** (continuity 192). **Eleven on the fourth of Embermonth, eleven in the return to the province, twelve in the guild hall on the ninth of Frostmonth because a man was given a vote, and the reeve entered both with the date of each and entered that a clerk in two hundred years could add one to the other and get a good sum of two true things and a lie.** **A count that goes up by one when a man is given a vote is a count measuring the wrong thing and nobody in that room can prove it and neither chapter pretends otherwise.** Bram Ottery is the proof and he is on the page and he is the man who asked for a vote and not an office and got it and is one of the eleven.

62. **A third hand four hundred miles off, behind a price this county cannot pay, and a roll in two hands with a third hand written on the end of it in a fair copy** (continuity 207, 208, 209). **Alis Merrow's counting house will hold a roll of persons in its press for a farthing of salt on a shelf, and the price of the shelf is eleven sheets that have lain there for eleven years for a creditor who did not want them, and the eleven sheets are in a press in a room with a door on a lane over a weigh-house in a fen county and nobody in either country knows they are wanted, and a term the county wrote itself forbids the errand.** **A court can take a thing out of a press that belongs to an office and cannot take one out of a press that belongs to a person, and that is Ivet Salter's whole sentence, and it is why the third hand must be a creditor's and not a registry's — and a registry press is the only holding in the world that the Crown can simply ask for.** **The bench could not put Merrow's minute where a Crown's man would find it, because there is no Crown's man in that county and there is nobody there to look, and it entered its own powerlessness in a book in a minute, and Merrow has to do it himself in his own city, at his own cost, or the argument is worth nothing in nine years.** Chapter 95 is where he does it.

63. **A register answers what was entered on the shelf and nothing else, and a creditor's press is the worst place in the world to keep a roll of persons and the only place where nobody can quietly take it out of the book** (continuity 208). **Both sentences are on the page, in Chapter 79, in letters, and both decide things.** Salter's is the volume's Volume 05 answer about roads in four words: *if you want a book that can be asked what a thing in it is for, then you want a court, and a court will give it to whichever of you asks loudest.* **Chapter 80's argument and Chapter 95's hall scene are the same argument and the same sentence, and Merrow has been making it for eleven years and is now the man doing it to his own city, and he asks that the argument be minuted.**

64. **A cell that is ruled and empty, in a copy of a shelf register in an office two hundred miles off, and the shape of it is the shape of the fifth space with no sixth and of the eighth ruled line that is the city's** (continuity 208). **A clerk of twenty-eight copied a whole leaf including four ruled cells at the bottom because her grandmother was struck off a parish roll once and never once said so, and on one line the county cell is ruled and empty, and a man four hundred miles off read it on a board with a wet cloth in his hand, stood there about a minute and a half, wrote one line in the back of a notebook and said nothing to anybody.** **The three blanks are the same blank. No character in Batch 0008 knows it, no character may say so, and the two documents are never in one room.** Chapter 92 is where a keeper of records finds a thing with a date in it, and the shape of that finding is a ruled cell that somebody did not fill, and she will not fill it either.

65. **A second notebook entry, four months after the first, in the same vocabulary, in tons, and he is going to build it anyway** (continuity 210). **Chapter 80 ends the block on the same sentence Chapter 69 ended on, in a different county, and the reader has now read the shape of it twice and knows what it is and nobody in the County of Ash knows and nobody in Merefen knows.** The witness line is identical: *a witness that is also the anchor is not a witness, it is a second reading of the same hand.* The figure is identical: two tons each, off a barrow of setts, with no reason of any kind. And the new line is the one that is the whole of him: **a man who has noticed a thing twice is not being careful, he is being consistent.** **Chapter 81 is where he says it out loud in a hall, four words into a fifth sentence, and a clerk four hundred miles off enters the arrest against him with the day and the hour, and nobody in the room understands what it means and he does and says nothing. Chapter 84 is where he finds out he was right, and the remedy is his and the damage is his and the method was correct and it is still him deciding for a room, and he may not notice before then.**

66. **The sum in two calendars, planted for the third time in his own hand** (continuity 210). *Four months, or thereabouts, which is the same thing at this rate, and I have not done the sum because the sum is in two calendars and I have not got both of them, and the one I have got does not have a week in it.* **The Chapter 69 notebook and the Chapter 70 spoken account and this one are the three instances, and the fourth is Chapter 92, where a keeper of records finds it with a date and not with a question.** The paper of the eighth of Embermonth is dated in a calendar this county cannot check and the letter of the sixteenth week is dated in a form the hand of the Ninebridge read out once and said *we cannot check one word of that*. **Four documents from two countries, two calendars, and one man who has now said three times on the page that he cannot do the sum. It is the volume's whole method and its whole danger and it is not tidied.**

67. **A struck man, an employed man, and a man whose kiln has shut are three different people, and the county's records can tell the difference for none of them** (continuity 206). **About forty men out, thirteen who do not go back, and six of the thirteen nobody in the county knows what happened to, entered as six lines with nothing against them and no reason on them — which is the same shape as the fourth hundred and fifty-six, and as the fifth space with no sixth, and as the eighth ruled line, and no character in this batch may put any two of them together.** **A man who is not on a column cannot be put on one, and a man who cannot be put on one cannot be given a vote, and a man who has not got a vote is not a witness, and a man who is not a witness cannot be restored.** That last chain is the whole of the County of Ash's moral life and it is now on the page in the county's own words, and Chapter 89 re-grounds the fired tiles at a cost and about thirteen men stay out and the number is written down.

68. **A county's price, footed in pence on a board, and the room that costed it instead of arguing** (continuity 204, 205). **Two hundred and six tiles at one in three fit is six hundred and eighteen into the kiln, fifteen rollings against five, a hundred and thirty-five cords against forty-five, ninety cords more, two thousand seven hundred pence for the wood and two hundred for the cutting, two thousand nine hundred pence, sixty marks one shilling eightpence, and about forty men whose day is lost — and the last two lines of that column are not the same and a man has had it entered that they are not to be added.** **It is the first page in that county about people and not about tiles, and the second line on it is a struck man asking for himself to be written down by somebody who was not him while he was still able to ask.**

69. **A charged rolling cannot wait, and the wedging shed is the room the rollkeeper kept a list out of, and reasons do not stay correct** (continuity 206). **She said it herself, in four sentences, to twenty men, and did not resign, and the reason she gives for not resigning is that a rollkeeper who resigns is a woman who has said the county's records are not worth a room, and this county has had four of those in nineteen years and the county did not stop.** **Chapter 98 supersedes the list and does not burn it and this is the price of that, and it was paid by the woman whose argument the clay agreed with.**

70. **The seal on the letter, cut off with a knife and dropped in the pit, and nobody said why** (continuity 211). **A hand laid flat on a book, on grey wax, on the envelope of four sheets from a city that is not on any map, brought to a shed at the north end of a bridge by a person who carried papers and did not stop.** The hand of the Ninebridge looked at it for about four seconds and cut it off. **No character in this batch names the device and no character in this volume may. It is the oldest object in the book after the bell and the reader has been carrying it since Chapter 10, and it is now on a letter, and the letter is in a drawer, and the drawer is not locked, because in that county a drawer with a lock in it is a thing somebody has to have a key to, and a key is a name.** Chapter 85 is the coping and Chapter 85 does not explain it either.

71. **What the Volume 02 close inherited and Batch 0008 did not touch, on purpose.** The narrow oath and the release. The cutwater against a pledge let go. The YR 302 out-of-place clearance entry. The Crown's letter in the room over a cooper, still the only document in the county with no chain of custody. The brine weights, still cited and not filed. The Hushwater Reach figure and the Hushwater figure, not printed together and not noticed. The two Hushwater women who are two women, and the three Hushwater women who are three. **The second severance's index leaf and press-mark** — press four, die eleven, second impression of the season — which is untouched in Block 3, and **the one cell in it that is ruled and empty is now mirrored, in a fair copy made four hundred miles off in a provincial registry, by a cell in a shelf register that is ruled and empty, and neither document is in the same country as the other and nobody has compared them.** Halven's endorsement cannot be withdrawn and is not withdrawn. **The second Gatehouse crossing does not answer and is not called, and the drift is not restated in this block, and the printed warning is still three weeks.** **All of these are still where the close put them and none of them may be spent in Block 4.**

## Threads opened by Chapters 81–90 (Batch 0009, Volume 02 Block 4)

Threads 1–20 of the close hand-off are the plan, threads 21–40 are Batch 0006's list, threads 41–54 are Batch 0007's and threads 55–71 are Batch 0008's; all four stand and none is rewritten. What follows is this batch's own list, in the order a writer needs it.

72. **The rite, and the arrest, and the two arrests in one book** (continuity 214, 215, 216, 217). **Four hundred and nine people through one room in eleven weeks, four sittings a day, and the office does not read the roll — it watches people answer. It is the best idea anybody has had in eleven months in either country and it is proposed in a hat in a hall of sixty, and it works, and four words into a fifth sentence a clerk of the counting house of the tolls of Bellweather-Living, who is not a Crown's man, enters the arrest against a name already on the leaf, with the day and the hour, in his own fair hand, in a private book in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists — and his reason in eleven seconds is that a book which cannot be got at is the only place in the world where a sentence a man said in a room four hundred miles off is safe to keep. Nobody in Merefen has a copy of either arrest and will not for a long time, and the counting house does not produce.** **The reading is read by Fennie Loam, 19, who is not to be blamed and not to be absolved, and it stopped in the seventh month because a man walked into a hall and took a page out of her hand, and the reading had begun two days before that with him standing at the end of the table.** **Nobody in this volume opens the second arrest. Volume 06 is the cut made on a document and it is a different instrument, and this one is a document, and Volume 02 does not turn it over.** The half a tile that crossed is **safe in a press that can be served, because there is nothing in it to take out** — and that is the answer Mara Vey's whole arc has been looking for and she does not see it for nine years and a later volume has it.

73. **A record a person can contradict is a record of that person, and the founding act of Ash was the attempt to build a claim** (continuity 218). **Fire everything, made in the fourth year after the register went, nine years to do, a record that could not be argued with, and it protected the county for nineteen years and it is the disease, because a county that cannot lose its people cannot hear them either and a man can obey a thing about himself for nineteen years and at the end of the nineteenth year not one word of his will be recognisable as his own.** The cure is the soft tile in three hands, none authoritative alone, **and it was being read too fast to survive, and the fastest line in the county was a list of restorations read aloud four times a day by a girl of nineteen.** Arrived at by a bridgewright on a stool in four days, in the trade's own words, in tons, and he got it exactly right and was wrong about who did it: **it is not the bound, and it is not the three hands, and it is not the anchor, and it is him — a promise is only as wide as the people who have agreed to it, and he had four hundred and nine of them and not one had agreed to anything, because he asked a hall at the fourth hour and a hall is not a person.** Pernel Quist arrives at the same thing from the other side, in a wet shed, about a minute, standing up. **Chapter 98 supersedes the list and does not burn it, and this is the argument for that, and it was paid for twice: by a man in a hat and by a man of fifty-eight who is still alive.**

74. **Ostry Vaile, 58, alive, employed, correct in three books, and does not know which kiln he fires** (continuity 219). **Restored in the ninth week of the rite and softening in the same week, and the softening is on the page as a sequence in four ordinary things — the order of the day, the trade, the hearth, the name — and no character assembles them, and a man who has spent nineteen years reading load paths never will because a person going back to being clay has no load path.** The roll is right about him: the mark on the tile agrees with the entry, both, not either, and the fourth column of a tile in this county has never once had to say which of Kilnholt's four kilns, because a tile is of a person and a kiln is not a person. **He has not been spoken to since the ninth week. His hearth has a fire in it on the nights the county can see it from a road. He is not dead, he is not struck, and no instrument in this county or in any other will do one thing about it, and the only person in the book who is not satisfied with that is the woman who reads for a living, four hundred miles off in a fen, who has not been told and is going to be told by a form.** **His want — to be left alone and uncopied — is the sentence the volume cannot argue with, was said in Chapter 66 and is not improved on here, and a man said it out loud in a kiln shed in nine sentences in the eighth month and was right.** **Chapter 100 is the last image and it is his: a man of fifty-eight who fired the tiles in that kiln last spring does not know which kiln he fires, in three books that are right about him, and the books are in three countries and not one of them is going to do anything about it.**

75. **The bell's absence, named, and the coping, and the one true thing** (continuity 221, 222). **There is no bell in the County of Ash and there has never been one, and the absence has a shape that only the man who sweeps can see, and the shape is the absence of a mark.** Garrick Dene, 64, has swept the coping of the second span for forty-one years and wants it lifted so that he can sweep under it, and his fearlessness is a trade thing and not a courage thing, and his want is a right and not a grievance. **He also names, from the bottom, why a roadkeeper in this county is *the hand* and not a steward: a hand that cannot be asked is not a hand, it is a person holding something, and there has never been anything in this county that could be asked, and that is why there has never been a hand that had to answer — not because we are better than other counties, because nothing has ever been put in front of us.** **Aren Kest said the true thing about the tile on the coping and the true thing cost him the only protection the lie had, because a man who does not know a thing cannot be asked what he has found out, and he has now said I do not know about it three times in one county in front of three people, and in nine years the question is going to be what a man off the road said on the coping of the second span, and there will be two answers and one of them is a lie.** The tile is not moved, not touched and not explained, and the eight inches under it are not swept, and the mark on it is described on the page only as a stamp off no die in this county. **Thread 48's debt is not settled; it is made larger. Chapter 99 is where the disposition schedule's county column opens halfway and is left, and the coping is not it.**

76. **A child drew a river, and the county has no such river, and there is nowhere to put a fact that is true and about nobody** (continuity 223). **Bel, four, of Kilnholt, pressed a bend, a mill with eight spokes and a weir of six slats into a soft tile in a kiln shed in the eighth month, in the ordinary way a child makes a mark on wet clay, and the woman who fired at Kilnholt saw her do it and did not go over and has been not going over about four months and said it only when the keeper of the roll asked her a question about a date.** He recognised it and did not say the name, and the room got the mark. **Nobody touched the clay, and the reason is the volume's mechanism: a soft tile is the only record a person can change, and nine people who work with clay every day worked out together that not one of them was going to be the last pair of hands on it.** **The rollkeeper's sentence is the most frightening thing in the volume: we have been looking at the wrong tile.** The arithmetic of Chapter 84 is now available to a reader and to nobody in the county: **a fired tile is the only record in this county a person cannot alter, and in about three years Bel's tile is going to be fired, and the mark is going to be a hand, and the river is going to be gone, and there is not going to be one thing in the world that says a woman of Kilnholt put a bend and a mill and a weir on the only account of herself that anybody will ever be able to take off her — and there is no instrument.** Thread 49 is paid and closed in the sense that the tile has now been looked at; the river is not explained in this volume and does not have to be. **The knowledge rule holds: the child is not on the roll and will not be for three years, and nobody has connected a river in a fen to a mark in wet clay, and no character in this block knows what a Sable is, and the river is not named in any room by anybody.**

77. **The county said out loud that it has no word for a refusal, and a hand is not a thing that answers** (continuity 224). Two sentences, in a path community's own voice, neither a paraphrase of anything said in Merefen: **we did not lose our register, we lost our refusal, a register is what you have instead** — from a woman of about sixty on three hearths whose fourth column says *wife*, the second entry in nineteen years in that office against a person by name that is not a removal; and **a hand answers for a road and a hand can be asked, and those are two halves and they do not go together, and nobody has ever asked me one single question about it in nine years** — from the hand of the Ninebridge, who has said no to about four people who wanted her to be the hand of something else and said yes in about four seconds to witnessing a stage, and who says the two are the same act. **The physician's four pages are read a second time and this time nobody misunderstands them, and the reason is the county's own doing and not a stranger's: a county that has been made into marks for nineteen years and has been read four times a day for eleven weeks cannot hear the second page of a finding without understanding it.** And **the two hundred books in two hundred sheds is the only civic work in this volume nobody has ever written down, and it is the physician's objection and it is unresolved, and the cord-cutter stood up in a room of fifty and said that the answer *yes* has already been said out loud in a kitchen in this county twice and he was not there for either, and asked somebody to explain what the difference is between a thing a man writes in his own hand in his own house and a thing a woman presses into a board with her own thumb — and nobody answered him, and the chapter is that not being answered.** Thread 44's argument is now on the page in the county's own voice and is not resolved in Chapter 98 either.

78. **A rule with no office behind it, a promised hand that is a list, and a roll in three hands of which two are hands** (continuity 225, 226, 227, 228). **The county's own fourth term went into its own book in the ninth month as a rule, in force from that hour, not conditional upon the road and not conditional upon any office, and the minute records that there is no clerk and that there is going to be no clerk, and that a rule with no office behind it is a thing that can be argued with — which is the whole of the argument for it and not one part against.** The county's own answer to term four is the re-reading broken into nine places over nine days by its own people, **and it is slower per man than the rite and it is better, and the difference is that the rite can only be run by one man and this can be run by about nine hundred people in this county who can read and count, and not one of the nine hundred has to be him.** **The road did not open on the ninth of the ninth month because the witness at Ash's end was struck in the eighth month and the county did not replace him in time, and the county's own slow answer is what emptied the line, and nobody in that room pointed that out; and Cobb Rennick, 52, refused in nine sentences, and there was no third candidate, and the bench does not hold a list of people who have been asked to stand somewhere because nobody has ever thought of asking it to. It did not fail. A man who has to write down that a road failed has found a way of making it somebody's fault.** The bench **lost** the vote on the eleven sheets, on the ground that **a promised hand that costs a man his season is a list with a nicer name on it**, and has entered that it cannot say whether the third hand is a hand and has resolved not to find out this year. **The third hand is still a letter at the end of the volume.** Pernel Quist refused for two days and then signed, **and her reason is that a roll that can be wrong is not a weaker roll, it is a roll that has to be read twice, and a roll that has to be read twice cannot be read by one man in a morning, and a roll that cannot be read twice cannot survive a person** — and she signed with a letter in it because the rule permits the third to be wrong, which is the physician's fourth page, and she asked for both halves of her own history entered and untidied. **Chapter 98 supersedes the list, and the roll in three hands is the mechanism, and the eleven sheets are Chapter 99's.**

79. **Ash is un-firing its own founding act, and the number is 254, and the count is not the other subtraction** (continuity 229). **A man with a ledger walked the walls and turned tiles over and got to two hundred and fifty-four tiles with the same hand on the back of two of them, out of two kilns, one village and half a wall, and it is a floor and he will not go past it, and a stack in the back of the Kilnholt shed stays shut. He is asked three times whether it is the four hundred and fifty-six and says no three times: one is a thing about a shortage and the other is a thing about a person, and the four hundred and fifty-six has not been divided, multiplied, worked back from or entered on his slate, and a later writer may not scale it into this.** The cost is footed on a board and copies: **thirteen men, five days, at five pence the day, three hundred and twenty-five pence, six marks three shillings and one penny; nine cords for the proof rolling, two hundred and seventy pence, five marks two shillings and sixpence; five hundred and ninety-five pence, twelve marks one shilling and seven pence** — **over two years, and the first of it is now, in the standing water, which is the county's name for the time between the second low water and the next first firing, when the Kiln is up over the working ground and nothing can be dug and nothing can be burned, and it is the only season in which a man who is out of work and a room with a floor are both available at the same time.** **This county has no Hearthmonth, and a list going north will name the season to a county that counts from its first firing.** The same motion has now been done four times — hers on a clamp bar, his on a garboard, a thumb in a dish forty times an hour for five days — and **none of the three has said one word about it to any of the other two and none of them is going to, because a movement is not a fact and if you put a movement in a book a man in nine years can ask you what it was for.** **Chapter 98 confirms the re-grounding and Chapter 100 is scheduled to say four hundred and fifty-six of the tiles are going to be ground back into clay — and that figure is main-world, and the county has two hundred and fifty-four and a floor, and nobody has told Merefen either number, and a later writer must not make a clerk in a fen scale one into the other.**

80. **The terms, the stage, and the object, and the one line a keeper of records cannot put down** (continuity 230, 231). **The five terms of the Second Door are printed in the Office's hand on the back of a form that has been in an unlocked drawer in a shed at the north end of a bridge since the seventeenth week, and the hand of the Ninebridge never read the small print because in this county a form is a thing a person brings in with them and not a thing anybody has to answer; and beneath the five, in a smaller type, as a standard of measurement and not as a term, *a road held, three weeks*, which the office cannot say whose figure it is.** Term three is **not an office** and is a town four hundred miles off working it out on a bench in nineteen years, and the same sentence is now printed at the foot of a form, and two people are carrying it about four hundred miles apart in two countries. **The stage came in a shed with a door on the side of it, with the only witness in the county who can be asked and a boy of sixteen copying the blanks, and there is no word for a stage in this county and there could not be, because a county that counts fires has not got a way of counting a man.** The object is **half a tile, a shade over ten ounces, because a whole tile is the account of a person and a half tile is the account of nothing**, and it is the only thing in this case that is safe in a press that can be served. The message is **a bill, four hundred words, itemised, checkable line by line, made by the county in about four minutes** — and it came down a line on a day the road did not open, **because a message is not a route, and the county does not know that.** **Line nine is the man at the kiln and the four reasons a keeper of records has for not entering it, and the third of the four is the office's own second wall rule, and the fourth is a promise written in Fallowmonth, and the reason for the promise is on the page and is not a sentence about the man: because a keeper of records who asks a question has already decided that the answer is not in the records she keeps, and a keeper who has decided that has stopped being a keeper and started being a person who wants something.** **The not asking is now a choice and the choice is the wound, and that is the series' Volume 2 relationship milestone, and the breach proper is Chapter 92 and is found with a date and not with a question, and it is not resolved in Volume 02 and is not to be resolved in Volume 03.**

81. **What Batch 0009 did not touch, on purpose.** **The narrow oath and the release. The cutwater against a pledge let go. The YR 302 out-of-place clearance entry. The Crown's letter in a room over a cooper, still the only document in that county with no chain of custody. The brine weights, still cited and not filed, with two day-book lines four lines apart. The Hushwater Reach figure and the Hushwater figure, not printed together and not noticed. The three Hushwater women who are three women, and the other three who are three. The second severance's index leaf and press-mark — press four, die eleven, second impression of the season — untouched, and the one cell in it that is ruled and empty is still mirrored four hundred miles off in a fair copy made in a provincial registry, and neither document is in the same country as the other and nobody has compared them. The two eighth ruled lines, which do not appear in this batch at all. Halven's endorsement, which cannot be withdrawn and is not withdrawn. The office's three dated lines of the thirteenth of Wolfmonth and the count of eleven that went to the province on the ninth of Frostmonth, both untouched and both waiting for Chapter 93.** **The second Gatehouse crossing has been made and the second route does not answer, and the drift is not restated in this block, and the printed warning is still three weeks.** **All of these are still where the close put them and none of them may be spent in Block 5, and the four blanks are not to be compared, and Chapter 92 is where a keeper of records finds one of them with a date and does not ask.**

## Threads opened by Chapters 91–100 (Batch 0010, Volume 02 Block 5) — and the three hooks out of the volume

**How to use this section.** Threads 1–81 are the record through Chapter 90. Threads **82 onward** are the record of the ten chapters that closed Volume 02, and the Volume 02 close is written against them. **Nothing in 82 onward may be closed by a repair pass, and the three hooks at the foot of this section are the volume's hand-off and are in `outline/volume-02.md` §11's order.**

82. **The breach, and the reason it is a breach and not a scene, and that it is not repaired** (continuity 238). **The fifth of Greenmonth, the seventh hour, a room over a weigh-house. A keeper of records finds the cost of the second crossing with the one instrument she cannot stop using, which is a date: he says in the ordinary way that a schedule of eight village re-readings was sent on the eighteenth of the ninth month of a county's own year, the fair hand writes it down, the fair hand goes to the day-book, and in about eleven seconds she finds that two dates in his account are dates of that county and not of this one, given in the wrong frame, twice, in one morning, without his noticing. A bridgewright would not have noticed. A keeper of records cannot not. The finding is not arrived at by asking him anything.** He tells her to write it down and she refuses, because a date is the only instrument she has that she cannot stop using and a date in a day-book in her own hand is a document about a man. **And then she rules a clean sheet, writes the twelve months of that county's year down one column, and tears it up in front of him, because two ends and a middle do not make a conversion, they make a number she made up, and every line under it would be a line she had invented, and in nine years a man would take a figure off it and use it and would not know he was using her.** The reason she gave in Fallowmonth for not asking is not spoken and is not written again; **the new thing on the page is the change — a protection is a thing done to somebody, and the not asking is now a wound, and a wound she is choosing not to open is worse than a question. She noticed the change on the twenty-ninth of Fallowmonth, which was the seventh day of the promise, wrote it down on the fifth of Wolfmonth, and the promise is two hundred and twenty-three days old at the fifth of Greenmonth, and the lateness is in the seven months since and not in the noticing.** **She does not ask him. That is the breach. The volume does not repair it, Volume 03 does not repair it, and Volume 04's chosen intimacy is bought with it.** A later writer may not resolve it in a room, may not have either of them say the word *wound* to the other, and may not turn it into a misunderstanding; it is not a misunderstanding, it is a decision entered eight times.

83. **The order of an ordinary morning, and the sentence spent in a yard, and that the estrangement is not healed** (continuity 236, 247, 248). **Six or seven things in an order, a particular Tuesday, a room over a cooper, a yard on Weir Street, eleven years of it. He can do every one of them and cannot do them in the order and does not know what the order was, and it is in a notebook in a coat and it is not in a form, and that is the concealment with the stance, and the concealment is the third time he has decided what a keeper of records may not be told.** The beat opens in Chapter 91 in a room and not a yard — **the brother watches five seconds of it and says nothing at all about any of them and shuts the door behind him for the second time in four years** — and is spent in Chapter 97, in a yard, in his own words, once, and may not be improved on: **I am not going to re-teach you your own brother.** With the fairness that makes it unarguable, and with the reason, and with the damage, which is permanent in this volume: **the sentence he said to his brother at a gate with his hand on a rope on the fourth of Hearthmonth, in daylight, with the yard going on round them, so that on the day he said nothing his brother would not think it was his brother, has been carried a hundred and ten days and found worse than the one he has just said, because that one was about a thing his brother would lose and this one is about a thing he is not going to do — and a man can be held to a thing he is not going to do.** **Nobody in that yard has the word brother in a scene, and no volume-03 writer may heal it in a scene, and no writer may have Tovan Kest re-teach him.** What is not ruined, and is the only relationship in Volume 02 currently getting better, is the yard: **a fortnight on the twenty-fourth of Greenmonth with Pei Tarrow still in it, the fourth of Hearthmonth having cost nobody a man, and the four days from the fifth of Wolfmonth still on the page in four entries in a hand his brother would know and not tidied into one.**

84. **A second count answered with a figure, a court that cannot be sued, and an office that can be** (continuity 240, 241). **The ninth of Greenmonth, the county town, a room over a court, a clerk putting a chair at a magistrate's table before anybody says anything.** The count is answered with **one thousand six hundred and nine, the number of people who have come through the door of that office in four years and a half, out of a day-book, and no name in it, and a claim number against the office and nothing against anybody.** **A magistrate of nine years beats the instrument in nine minutes with the form's own construction — a count of persons is a return of a figure, a figure is not a person, no person is in default, a thing against which nobody is in default is not refused and is not enforced, and the instrument is therefore spent the moment it is answered — and is frightened of her own answer, and says why in a room, and refuses to have it minuted: every argument she has ever had with that office is built on the fact that this court is not a person, and in about four years somebody is going to make it a person, and the first county in this kingdom to build one will not be a madman, it will be a woman of forty-five who is right about the mechanism.** **And the county council resolves that the Archive is a body corporate, on a form whose printed space under *Function of the office* is filled in the founding's own thirteen words and whose space above the ruled line — the space for a thing's *name* — is left empty.** Nobody in the room says it; the chapter does not say it; Lysa Fenn says it once, in a yard, at the sixth hour, to one person, and is not answered. **The count is now the mechanism by which that office can be closed without anybody's name ever being written down anywhere, and the volume's political victory is still that no name has been written down in any Crown's hand in this county since the twenty-seventh of Fallowmonth, and it is still how the Crown got further in seven months than it got in eleven years.** A later writer may not have the figure disputed, may not have it broken, and may not have the body corporate turned into a thing anybody has yet used.

85. **Sabin Dain, and a witness as the only instrument that cannot be withdrawn, and the thing she will become** (continuity 242). **The twelfth of Greenmonth, the county town, on foot from the county post, in a plain good coat, with a leather bag.** She did not come to be answered, she came to be asked, **and she asked to be recorded before anybody offered her a chair, with her name spelled with the whole of it, and the clerk of the county council wrote it, read it back, and let her correct one word.** **Being recorded is the most frightening thing anybody can do to an officer of that office and the only thing that makes an officer answerable, and she has been nearly asked four times in three counties in eleven years and has spent nine months wishing in each case that somebody had gone on asking.** Her want is unchanged and is not about Aren: **that no family in this country is ever again removed from a record by a name nobody entered.** **And she says the sentence, and it is a warning and not a threat and not an offer: a figure has no name in it; in four years there will be a number in a file with no name attached to it and that number will be about this county and nobody will be able to say whose hand made it; not because the hand has been concealed, because a figure does not need one; and it is not coming because anybody thought of it in a room with a clock on the wall, it is coming because it is cheaper than names and there is not one instrument in this kingdom that can make it more expensive.** She is a woman with a form and a good filing system and a horror of unnamed things, **which is Mara Vey's position mirrored, and in Volume 04 she joins an order that has learned to remove names rather than complain about them, and a reader who was paying attention at Chapter 55 has seen this already.** **She refuses to discuss the *Keeper* column in a gateway, and when he does not press her she says thank you, and that is the whole of their relationship in Volume 02, and it is the chapter's protection and not an accident.** She is a witness in a county's minute and not in a register, and she has said that a witness is a figure in a book that answers a question for as long as the book is dry, and that it will be the only name in the file, and that she will have earned it and it will not help her at all. **She does not touch the marginal instruction at the foot of the R.C. series, does not notice it, and no character in Chapters 91–100 does. Volume 03 is where Aren learns what it is being read as.**

86. **A press-mark proves the book and not the man, and the second severance does not open** (continuity 243). **The sixteenth of Greenmonth, in Merefen, from four leaves walked in on the back of a toll-taker in a basket who does not ask what they are.** The ninth line of an index and the first line carry **press four, die eleven and two impressions of one season; the ninth is the only one of the nine marked *answered*, eleven days after the tender; the ninth's county cell is ruled and empty; and the eight that are of a valley in this kingdom are all marked *returned* and none is marked answered.** The signature on the reading is a clerk who is the grandson of the only frightened hand in four hundred leaves, and the whole of what he says is nine words: **I can tell you the press and I can tell you the die and I have had both leaves in my two hands at the same time under a lamp and I am not able to tell you the hand.** **The finding: a press-mark does not lie about a hand the way a name does, because a press-mark is a fact about an object and a name is a fact about a person, and a person can be got at and the getting at him can be refused, and he can be alive or dead, and that man has been alive the entire eleven years, which is the one fact about him nobody in either country has been able to use.** **The name of Edric Hunn is not written anywhere in Chapters 91–100, and the join is not stated once, and nobody in this country learns what the ninth line is.** Alis Merrow's entry is in his own hand on his own paper at his own cost on his own motion and not on a fee, entered as produced by a creditor's office in a matter in which no court in this county has jurisdiction, **and it is the first document in the series held by a person in a path**, and the four-line argument is minuted in his own words and the minute is asked to be put where a Crown's man will find it in about nine years. **The four conditions on a wrapper are broken, all four, on purpose, and are not written down as broken — a person breaks a condition and gets away with it not by denying it but by not putting it next to the thing.** **The second severance does not open inside Volume 02. It opens in Chapter 894, on a form, when a clerk in a provincial office puts two documents side by side, and the reader has been holding both halves for thirty chapters and has not been told.**

87. **A disclosure and an endorsement on one page, and nobody is charged** (continuity 244). **The twenty-second of Greenmonth, a Monday.** Halven's four paragraphs, dated the fifth of Embermonth, YR 312, at the tenth hour, on his own motion and not on a fee, about a volume that was not on its shelf and whose removal carries no name, office, authority, destination, fee or initial, and which he knew was gone for six years; **a schedule for three bells of which two are removed with no office against either and the third is entered retained and is the bell four hundred yards up a road in this county; and a county column of dispositions running to eleven other counties with a word in it this county does not use for anything else, and I am not going to say the word, and I have said it in a room once, to two people, and one of them wrote it in a book and one of them did not, and I have not been able to find out since which of them did.** Beside it, **Memorandum of endorsement, the office of the marshal's emergency office, the fourth of Embermonth, YR 312, the writ of enquiry on the reeve's records discharged, number 4 of the season** — **the first document in the case in which the Crown's shield and the Crown's payment for a book are in one paragraph, which happened because two officers of one office at two desks nine miles apart each did the ordinary thing their own desk did and one of them did not know the other existed.** **Halven came two hundred miles on his own money and stood by a press for an hour and a quarter and nobody asked him one question, and he counted twice, and asked for that to be entered against a person, and was told the reason there is a page cannot be entered.** **Nobody is charged, and he keeps the post — he was in the fourth press two hundred miles off on the twenty-third of Greenmonth and will be in it on the twenty-fourth — and the endorsement stands and is not withdrawn because the office that gave it has no procedure for withdrawing an endorsement and will not build one inside a quarter, and the clerk who would have to build it has four desks.** He is called back in Volume 11 and accepts restitution and archive labour and is barred from office for ever, and the reason he can be is that there is one document in the world in which it says he did it. **The marshal is told nothing and learns it in a file, and the marshal is not surprised by anything, because nothing has reached him.**

88. **A day-book is not a file, and a file is not a record, and a record is not a roll, and a post is a cage** (continuity 245, 246, 247). **Alish Renn, 30, a second copyist on a warrant of Mara Vey's own that is not entered in the press, and she is right about all three, and nobody improves it.** Her want, in a room, because there is no other room: **to be a keeper with a post and her name in the day-book.** A name in a day-book against a person is the only thing in that office that is not a record, and it is the only job in the county that is about a person instead of about a document, and **Mara did not give it to her and did not explain it for a year, and Sarn Oyelaran worked it out in nine seconds and got the answer to a warrant for a third post that has been in the day-book since the sixteenth of Greenmonth, YR 312 and has not been signed.** **A copyist's warrant that is not in the press is a thing a person has in a drawer, and a man with a pair of shears and forty minutes could take a leaf out of the day-book, and that is how half the things in this kingdom have gone missing.** And the other cage: **the copying post at three shillings a week, sixteen weeks and two days, unclaimed, twelve marks and tenpence in the county's hands with no post for it to be in, and a post is a place a man is sent for and also a place a man is sent away from, and she wrote the first half of that out in Fallowmonth in four lines and did not see the second half coming and is not going to pretend now that she did.** A later writer may not have the post come back to him and may not have the twelve marks and tenpence paid.

89. **A bench asked three times and said yes three times, and a bill with a line in it that had nothing against the line** (continuity 246, 247). **The twenty-fourth of Greenmonth, a Wednesday, the road shut, a fortnight, eleven tons of stone in four carts, the yard at a fortnight with Tarrow still in it.** The third asking took about six minutes and the reeve asked that the minute carry that Tovan Kest said in the fourth of Frostmonth that a bench asked three times and saying yes three times is not a bench, it is a subscription, **and that the bench knows it and asked anyway, because a bench that votes for a thing in spite of the fact that it is a subscription is a bench.** The trust's account for the absence **foots in pence on the page before the total is spoken: 1,626 + 372 + 216 + 204 = 2,418 pence, fifty marks one shilling sixpence, and the fifth line has no figure against it at all and is not in the total because there is nothing in it to total.** The scour under the nose of the third pier is a washtub and has been since the sixth of Mudmonth and nobody has read it, and the line is read on the twenty-fourth at the fourth hour, and **on the twenty-ninth the county's surveyor pays the guild's man with the levels six pence out of a box for a written report and tells nobody, and it is the smallest thing anybody in this county has paid for in two years and the only one anybody in either country has been glad of.** **The line with nothing in it now has a figure against it. Do not let a later volume un-read the scour and do not let anyone cost the eleven tons and the fortnight in a way that makes the bench's third asking look cheap.**

90. **The list is superseded and not burned, the three hands, and the third hand is still a letter** (continuity 249). **The county's own bench withdrew the magistrate's instrument in a room, with the physician's four-year-old finding read out and the three hands named, and the superseding is entered as a fact and not as a defeat**, and the magistrate's testimony is four sentences and includes **I want the county to hear the word stranger from me and not from him, because he is a man off a road and I am the magistrate of this county** and **I am not sorry about the fever; I was wrong about the names and I have never once been wrong about the clay.** **Ash's dead stay Ash's, in the shortest line in the county's own book, with no claim upon them and none to come.** **254 fired tiles are being ground back into clay by hand at a cost of twelve marks one shilling and sevenpence over two years on a schedule, in the standing water, with a bench; about thirteen men are out of work and the number is written down; the softening has slowed and not stopped, six boards against forty, entered with the hour and no opinion; and a rule with no office behind it that the whole of it is a person struck may answer.** The three hands: **the rollkeeper's office at the assizes, which is one room and does not move; the hand of the Ninebridge, which is a tile-cutter and has been asked about and has never been asked; and a creditor's press in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists, entered as a transaction. A person is restored when two of the three agree and removed when two of the three agree otherwise and the third is allowed to be wrong, and the bench has entered that it is unable to say whether the third is a hand and has resolved not to find out this year.** **Nobody in either country resolves the third hand and nobody is going to.** And the sentence that came down a line in a hand that is not a clerk's: **she does not know whether re-grinding what was fired is a good idea, and she is going to do it anyway, and she would like somebody to write down that she said so, in a book that is not hers, and she has not been told whether it can be.**

91. **A holding made in the country where the register is, a frightened signature, and no column for a want** (continuity 251). **The twenty-seventh of Greenmonth, a Saturday, a market day, the fourth hour.** *Received: the roll of persons of a county of nine hundred and forty-one hearths, in three hands, of which the third is this house. Consideration: one farthing of salt.* **The holding is made in the country where the register is, and the price is a farthing of salt because that is the smallest thing the office has ever bought anything with and it keeps a column for it.** The buyer is **the salt merchant's brother, in a house on a coast, who has wanted a canal wharf for nine years, who does not know what he has bought and thinks it is a debt, and who is not a villain** — and he is the brother of the man who owns the building the day-book is in, and the reason Mara Vey is not going to ask him about it is four words long: **I work next door.** **A person who owns a room cannot be the person who finds out what is in it, and that is not a rule anybody has ever printed and it is the rule that office runs on.** The four-line argument is minuted in the creditor's own words and he signs the last of them, **and a clerk has entered in the same document that the steward is a fool for putting the minute in the ordinary place, because a copyist is allowed to add an opinion to a copy and a steward is not.** **Ansel Tarrant, twenty-one years in that house, is the only new name in this business that has ever been read out in this county, and nobody asked for it**: the clerk who signs the four-hundred-leaf register in the hand his grandmother signed it in, the second frightened signature in four hundred leaves, who asked for a sentence about her to be written down in a copy and for nothing else, **and who said that a book four hundred leaves long that cannot be got at by anybody is the only place either of them has ever been frightened where it was safe to be.** He is entered in a day-book in a fen county against a person, with a claim number against nothing, **and no want is stated for him because there is no column for one in a creditor's book, in a county's roll of persons, or in a day-book over a weigh-house, and the absence of a column is the point and no volume may sentimentalise him.**

92. **The tile on the coping, and the half tile, and the tile in a press, and the three objects** (continuity 237, 251, 252). **The tile on the coping of the second span at the Ninebridge is not touched, not looked at and not explained in Chapters 91–100, and no character names the device on it, and it has been on that coping for forty-one years and its maker is dead and it was put there before the roll was begun.** It is named once in this batch, in Chapter 99, in a room four hundred miles from it, as a thing a keeper has known about for ten months because a man off a road told her he did not know what it was, and as a thing the county's own books do not mention, and the chapter's last line about it is that a thing you do arithmetic on is not a thing you have looked at. **It is the oldest object in the book after the bell and the reader has been carrying it since Chapter 10, and it is the third hook out of the volume.** **The half tile is the object of the fourth term: a fired tile, six inches by four, broken across the middle, the half that travels has nothing on it at all, a shade over ten ounces, and it is the account of nobody, and a whole fired tile cannot be re-pressed and is therefore a claim about a person, which is the disease.** The first went north in the bag of the hand of the Ninebridge on the fourth of that county's ninth month; **he has no way of finding out whether it is in a press in Merefen and is not going to ask, and the one in his bag is a second he made himself behind a kiln with a saw mark on the back of it, and the reason there are two is that he could not think of a way to ask whether the first had arrived, and a man who makes two of a thing to avoid asking a question has already decided what the answer is.** It is in a press with a claim number against nothing. **These are the only two objects in the case that a court can be served with and take nothing out of, and neither of them explains anything, and the reason the county made one is the same sentence that made the roll.**

93. **The eighth ruled line, the brine weights, and the reason that changed** (continuity 250). **The eighth ruled line in the served schedule of the eighth of Fallowmonth, YR 312 — seven villages by name, `sum not stated`, eight ruled lines, the eighth ruled and empty — was looked at for the third time on the twenty-sixth of Greenmonth, in the eighth month after the first look and the seventh month after the second, and was not filled in**, and the entry says the wanting is not the same wanting and the reason is not given, because **a keeper who explains a difference to herself in a day-book has made a document about a person, and there is no person in this and that is the point.** **The city is four thousand by the Crown's figure and has been for eleven years, the seven villages are seven hundred and twenty by name, the two are never added together anywhere in this case, and the city is still not on the served schedule at the close of the volume.** **The brine weights are still cited and not filed, the two day-book lines are five days apart in one week of Fallowmonth and are read together, and the reason is written out in full for the first time in the batch because the reason has changed: a ledger is a claim, and this office has learned this year, from a county four hundred miles off, that a claim about people is not a record of them.** The roll of the dead built out of them is not built and is not going to be built this year, and the not building is entered, and **Chapter 892's.**

94. **The state of the case at the close of the hundredth chapter, in one place** (continuity 252, 253, and the Batch 0010 entry in `state/batch-summaries.md`). **Ostry Vaile, 58, alive, employed, correct in three books, does not know which kiln he fires, has not been spoken to since the ninth week of a room at the assizes, has a fire in his hearth on the nights the county can see it from a road, and no instrument in this kingdom or any other will do one thing about it.** He is a number on the page four times over and is a person in none of them, and **the woman who reads for a living has written the whole of it down with the hour on it, against nobody, in a day-book over a weigh-house, four pence a year, and there is no name on the line and no opinion on the line, and the pen is down, and the last line of Volume 02 is not a sentence about him.** **Aren: Stage 2, the Second Door, two routes held, two carryovers of which he cannot say which path gave him which, one message and it must be a list, one object under a pound, and the second route does not answer when it is called; on the guild roll, with a vote, not holding the crossing, not voting, and on the deck of the Sedge Reach on the lowest water of the year with a tool bag counting load points; carrying a notebook in a coat with a morning in it; and the breach with the keeper of records open and unrepaired and not to be repaired in Volume 03.** **Mara: a keeper of records, one room over a weigh-house with a door on a lane, four pence a year, two rooms' work, a body corporate since the ninth of Greenmonth, two unserved books and a press with a half tile in it, eight entries of the not writing in one month, and one line in a fair hand about a man at a kiln that she has not asked about in four months and is not going to.** **The bell is on the Old Toll bridge with a band of forty-one rivets and a dull red seam in it and is not his and he does not ring it, and the far end of the line keeps the same day on a coping on a river this county does not have.** **The Crown's instruments in this county: four, all of them lawful or unanswerable, and two dated forms from the Office of Provincial Registration with a figure in each and no name in either, and a count of one thousand six hundred and nine that expired on the ninth of Greenmonth, and no name written down in any Crown's hand since the twenty-seventh of Fallowmonth.** **Ash: nine hundred and forty-one hearths, a roll in three hands of which the third is a letter, a rule with no office behind it, about thirteen men out with the number written down, 254 tiles going back into clay in the standing water at a cost on a board, and a softening that has slowed and has not stopped.** **Merefen: the Old Toll crossing in trust in abeyance with the tolls suspended and a dressed block and forty yards of tarred hemp in the abutment and no glass; the Sedge Reach shut for a fortnight with eleven tons going down and a nose out of the water for the first time since the sixth of Mudmonth; a bench of nine asked three times and saying yes three times and entered that it knows the difference; a yard at a fortnight with four men and a boy; and a bill of fifty marks one shilling and sixpence with a line in it that had nothing against it and now has six pence of report against it.**

95. **The three hooks that leave the volume, in the order `outline/volume-02.md` §11 gives them, and what the next volume inherits.** *These three are the volume's hand-off and are not to be spent inside Volume 02 or restated in a way that improves on what is on the page.*

**Hook one — the disposition schedule's county column, and eight counties with a stone in a field.** **On the page in Chapter 99, as a column and not as a revelation: a copy of a copy of a copy of a county column running to eleven other counties, of which eight have a boundary oath renewed in public on a stone, in a year anybody there can name, by men who are still alive and can be asked what they swore and to what and in whose name, and three have nothing in the leaf at all.** It can be seen by exactly two people in the world, a registrar who sold the book and a keeper who has a copy of a page of it, and **neither of them can say what an oath has to do with a severance.** It is entered as a question and not as a finding, with the hour, against nobody, no opinion, and the eight are left where they are. **The next volume is a border oath in the Hearth Marches killing the soil and trapping thousands of farmers in a path whose water route is failing, and a Marches council that refuses outside command, and Tovan Kest's bridge crew caught inside it.**

**Hook two — the marginal instruction that manufactures a keeper and that nobody in this volume understood, and the officer who wrote the letters.** **Untouched in Chapters 91–100. No character in those ten chapters mentions it, refers to it, explains it or notices it, and Chapter 94 is written specifically so that it cannot be noticed.** The form is on the page; the instruction is in an office's hand on the reverse; **nobody in either country can say whose figure a road held, three weeks is, and the ratio of the error to the absence is not a constant, and Chapter 91 states the sixty-seven-day error once and then never again.** **Sabin Dain wrote the letters and will write more of them, and in Volume 03 she offers a road warden's rank and a sealed personal record in exchange for closing the eastern paths, and she is a figure in a county's minute and not in a register and she is glad of that.**

**Hook three — the tile on the coping of the second span at the Ninebridge.** **A hand's width, with a rope on it in two loops and no yoke, carrying the device of a hand laid flat on a book, which nobody in the County of Ash knows what it is, and whose maker is dead, and which has been on that coping since before the roll was begun.** **It is not explained in Volume 02 and no character may explain it, and it is the oldest object in the book after the bell and the reader has been carrying it since Chapter 10.** It appears once in Chapters 91–100, in a room four hundred miles from it, in Chapter 99, as a thing in a column of a copy of a purchase that is in none of the columns, and the sentence about it is that a keeper has known about it for ten months because a man off a road said he did not know what it was, and that the county's own books do not mention it, and that **a thing you do arithmetic on is not a thing you have looked at.**

---

## Threads the Volume 02 close carries into Volume 03

**Written by the close, cross-referenced to `outline/volumes/volume-02-close.md` and to continuity items 255–269. No thread below is closed. Threads 82–95 all stand as thread 95 records them, and none of them is closed by this close except by saying on the page that a thing was closed and where — and no such page exists yet for any of them.** The close did not repair the breach, did not resolve the second severance, did not fill the eighth ruled line, did not file the brine weights, and did not touch the tile on the coping.

**The three hooks, in `outline/volume-02.md` §11's order, are the volume's hand-off and are now also continuity items 259, 260 and the close's §9.**

- **Hook one — the county column of a schedule of dispositions, eleven counties, eight with a boundary oath renewed in public on a stone in a year anybody there can name, three with nothing in the leaf at all.** Thread 91, item 251, close §9. **It is not a revelation and it is not a prophecy and a column is not a cause.** It is Volume 03's central question and the close has fixed what the answer is, in item 259 and in `outline/volume-03.md` §8 Block 4: **an oath renewed in public by men who are still alive is the only durable consent a rural county can produce and also the only document in the countryside that names a body, and a body can be served; and the eight stones are not eight of the same instrument, because some of them fix a count and a boundary about a share of water is a claim and not a boundary.** The pattern may not be resolved before Chapter 141, nobody may use the phrase *Culling Order* in connection with it, and no Crown's man may explain it.
- **Hook two — the marginal instruction on the reverse of Form R.C. 4, and the officer who wrote the letters.** Thread 85, item 260, close §9. **Untouched, unexplained and unmentioned through fifty chapters, and Chapter 94 was written specifically so that it could not be noticed.** Volume 03 is where he learns what it is being read as, and he learns it **out of a file and not out of a person.** It may be printed, quoted in a return, cited in a file and refused about in a gateway; **it may not be put in anybody's mouth as an explanatory sentence, and Sabin Dain may not be the person who explains it and may not change her want.** She offers a road warden's rank and a sealed personal record for closing the eastern paths, and the recruitment is revealed by her, in writing.
- **Hook three — a tile on a coping of the second span at the Ninebridge, a hand's width, with a rope on it and no yoke, whose maker is dead and which has been on that coping since before the roll was begun.** Thread 92, item 266, close §9. **It is the oldest object in the book after the bell and it is not explained in Volume 03 either.** No character may name the device, be told what it is, or be told what it is for. **No Volume 03 batch may schedule an explanation, a flashback, an origin, a maker or a deathbed**, and if a batch finds itself building a scene whose engine is the tile, that batch has misread the volume it is in. It is now also on the far side of a road that closes in Block 4.

**The rest of what the close carries, in the order a later writer will need it.**

- **The breach. Thread 82, items 238 and 263, close §8 item 1.** Open. **Not repaired, not to be repaired in Volume 03, and Volume 04's chosen intimacy is bought with it.** No apology, in a scene, an aside or a document; no question asked about the morning; no misunderstanding; and **the three rules entered in a day-book in Volume 03 are a procedure built around a concealment and not an end to it, and the volume must say so on the page in a document.** The third rule ends *and a habit is a thing a county does to itself when nobody is looking*, and that clause is a sentence out of a fair copy of a county bench's minute four hundred miles off, and neither of the two of them credits it to anybody.
- **The order of an ordinary morning, and the spent sentence. Thread 83, items 236, 247 and 248.** Spent, in a yard, in his own words, once, and not to be improved on. **No Volume 03 writer may heal the estrangement in a scene and no writer may have Tovan Kest re-teach him.** What Volume 03 adds is that the yard took a contract in a county four hundred miles off because a yard has to eat, and that the crew went in on the county's own calendar four days before he did, and that nobody in Merefen told anybody anything.
- **The second severance. Thread 86, item 243, close §7.2.** **The join is not stated once in fifty chapters and Edric Hunn's name is not written anywhere in Chapters 91–100.** It opens in **Chapter 894**, on a form, in a provincial office, when a clerk puts two documents side by side. **No Volume 03 writer may put a name in a column, may join the two halves, and may not have the marshal appear; the marshal is told nothing and learns what he learns in a file.**
- **A figure about a county with no name in it, and an office that can now be served. Thread 84, items 240 and 241.** **1,609 is not to be disputed, not to be broken, and the body corporate is not to be turned into a thing anybody has yet used.** Both are opened again in Volume 03 — a second proposed count, of oath-bound persons in a border county — and **no name is written in a Crown's hand for the second time.**
- **A man of fifty-eight who does not know which kiln he fires. Thread 74, item 252.** The volume's last movement and **the last line of Volume 02 is not a sentence about him.** **He is not visited in Volume 03 and no Volume 03 chapter may stage him on a page**, and the Marches' own letter to that county is the volume's next-volume question and is not answered inside Volume 03.
- **The eight county cells, and the four ruled cells, and the sixth line. Thread 93, item 266.** **No two of the volume's five blanks may be in one room, in one chapter or in one sentence, and no character may say any two of them are the same.** The eighth ruled line in the served schedule **is not looked at a fourth time in Volume 03 and no character refers to it**, and the city is still four thousand and the seven villages are seven hundred and twenty and the two are never added.
- **The brine weights. Thread 93, item 250.** **Still cited and not filed. Nothing in Volume 03 may file them, exhibit them, or begin the roll**, which is Chapter 892's, and the reason has changed and is now on the page: a ledger is a claim, and a claim about people is not a record of them.
- **The two unserved books, and the half tile, and the body corporate's door.** Item 262. All three are Volume 03's material and **none of them explains anything.**
- **The banned sums, the two Hushwater figures, and the money rule.** **Four thousand and seven hundred and twenty are never added; Hushwater Reach's near sixty and Hushwater's eighty are never printed together and no character notices the names match; every money column is added in pence and footed on the page before the total is said aloud; the Chapter 10 column's stated total of eleven marks and fourpence is canon and is not re-derived, and nothing may be scaled out of it.** The Chapter 97 bill is the model and its arithmetic is on the page.
- **The Stage 3 decision. Item 257.** **Stage 3 is not reached in Volume 03 and must not be, and the cut is named, refused, dated and not explained, and Volume 06's beat is held intact.**
- **The three known arithmetic defects, recorded so that no later phase treats them as fresh.** Item 263, the promise's age, a month out in the prose and in five state files, with the shape canon. Item 264, Chapter 70's eight weeks, which stands. Item 265, Petch's dates, where the chapter's are canon and item 161's are not reachable. And item 267, the standing rule: **a later phase states a figure, checks it, and records the check, and a state file is the least reliable document in this repository.**

## Threads Batch 0011 carries into Batch 0012

**Written by the Volume 03 Batch 0011 phase, cross-referenced to `outline/volume-03.md` §8 Block 2 and to continuity items 270–287. No thread below is closed. Threads 82–95 all stand exactly as the Volume 02 close records them and none of them is touched in Chapters 101–110 except where a chapter says on the page that a thing happened.**

- **The breach. Thread 82, items 238 and 263, and item 283.** Open. **Not repaired, not to be repaired in any scene, aside, document or apology in Volume 03, and Volume 04's chosen intimacy is bought with it.** What Chapter 106 added and what may not be undone: **three rules in the day-book, signed, in a book nobody can serve, in a building that can be sued, and the page beneath them says in the keeper's own hand that they are a procedure built around a thing that is not being said and are not a cure.** The third rule's final clause is a habit, a county, and nobody looking, and neither of the two of them credits it to anybody, and she has the four lines in her own hand and does not say so. **He gives her the numbers and not the morning. She does not ask.
